), with
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{cofact}})
instead of
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{cofact}})
.

Definition 8.4

(Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fact}})
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space
X
. Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
be a measured subgroupoid. We say that the pair
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
satisfies
Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fact}})
if the following three properties hold.

(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fact}})_{1}

The subgroupoid
\mathcal{F}
is of infinite type.

(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fact}})_{2}

There exist measured subgroupoids
\mathcal{Q}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{Q}_{n}
of
\mathcal{G}
, with
n\geq 1
, and such that
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{Q}_{j})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{cofact}})
for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, and there exists a countable Borel partition
X^{*}=\sqcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
of a conull Borel subset
X^{*}\subseteq X
such that for every
i\in I
, one has
\mathcal{F}_{|X_{i}}=(\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{|X_{i}}
.

(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fact}})_{3}

For every subgroupoid
\mathcal{Q}
of
\mathcal{G}
such that
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{Q})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{cofact}})
, and every positive measure Borel subset
U\subseteq X
, there exists a positive measure Borel subset
V\subseteq U
such that either
\mathcal{F}_{|V}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{|V}
or
(\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{Q})_{|V}
is trivial.

Replacing the use of
7.9
,
7.9
by
8.3
in the proof of the first point of Lemma
7.11
,
lemma

7.11
we obtain the following statement.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
G
be a reducible graph product of countably infinite groups over a finite simple graph
\Gamma
with trivial clique factor. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space
X
, equipped with an action-like cocycle
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to G
. Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
be a measured subgroupoid.

Then
(\mathcal{F},\rho)
is of factor type within
(\mathcal{G},\rho)
if and only if
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fact}})
. ∎

For the following statement, we refer to Definition
7.12
,
7.12
from the previous section for the definition of Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{vert}})
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
G
be a reducible graph product of countably infinite groups over a finite simple graph
\Gamma
with no transvection and no partial conjugation. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space
X
, and let
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to G
be an action-like cocycle. Let
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
be a measured subgroupoid.

Then
(\mathcal{V},\rho)
is of vertex type if and only if there exist a measured subgroupoid
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
such that
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fact}})
, and a countable Borel partition
X^{*}=\sqcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
of a conull Borel subset
X^{*}\subseteq X
such that for every
i\in I
, one has
\mathcal{V}_{|X_{i}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{|X_{i}}
, and the pair
(\mathcal{F}_{|X_{i}},\mathcal{V}_{|X_{i}})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{vert}})
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{k}
be the decomposition of
\Gamma
as a join of irreducible subgraphs (well-defined up to permutation of the factors), and let
G=F_{1}\times\dots\times F_{k}
be the corresponding decomposition of
G
as a direct product (since
G
is reducible,
k\geq 2
). Notice that as
\Gamma
is transvection-free, no
\Gamma_{i}
is reduced to a point, in other words
G
has trivial clique factor.

We first assume that
(\mathcal{V},\rho)
is of vertex type. Up to a countable partition of the base space
X
, and up to conjugating the cocycle, we will assume that
\mathcal{V}=\rho^{-1}(G_{v})
for some
v\in V\Gamma
. Let
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
be such that
v\in V\Gamma_{j}
. Let
\mathcal{F}=\rho^{-1}(F_{j})
. By Lemma
8.5
,
lemma

8.5
the pair
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fact}})
. In addition,
\Gamma_{j}
has no transvection and no partial conjugation (Remark
2.1
,
2.1
), so it is strongly reduced by Lemma
6.3
,
6.3
. It thus follows from Lemma
7.13
,
lemma

7.13
(applied to the groupoid
\mathcal{F}
and to the cocycle
\rho
, viewed as an action-like cocycle
\mathcal{F}\to F_{j}
), that
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{vert}})
.

Conversely, assume that there exist a subgroupoid
\mathcal{F}
as in the lemma – up to reasoning on each subset of the partition, we will assume that
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{vert}})
. Since
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fact}})
, Lemma
8.5
ensures that, up to a countable Borel partition of
X
and passing to a conull Borel subset, we have
\mathcal{F}=\rho^{-1}(F_{j})
for some
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. Since
\Gamma_{j}
is transvection-free and strongly reduced, and
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{vert}})
, it follows from Lemma
7.13
,
lemma

7.13
that
(\mathcal{V},\rho)
is of vertex type, as desired.
∎

We are now in position to complete the proof of the main proposition of the section.

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

Let
G
and
H
be two graph products of countably infinite groups over finite simple graphs that split (possibly trivially) as joins of strongly reduced transvection-free finite simple graphs on at least
2
vertices.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid equipped with two action-like cocycles
\rho_{G}:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow G
and
\rho_{H}:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow H
.

Let us first show that
G
is reducible if and only if
H
is.

If
G
is reducible there exists a subgroupoid
\mathcal{Q}
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
(\mathcal{Q},\rho_{G})
is of co-factor type within
(\mathcal{G},\rho_{G})
. By
8.3
,
8.3
, the pair
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{Q})
verifies
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{cofact}})
and therefore
H
is also reducible.

If
G
is reducible, the conclusion of Proposition
8.1
,
8.1
is now an immediate consequence of Lemma
8.6
,
lemma

8.6
, as we have characterized subgroupoids of
\mathcal{G}
of vertex type with respect to some action-like cocycle
\rho
, using a groupoid-theoretic property that does not refer to the cocycle
\rho
.

And if
G
is irreducible, the conclusion of
8.1
,
8.1
follows from
lemma

7.13
,
lemma

7.13
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

By Corollary
6.4
, if a finite simple graph
\Gamma
is transvection-free and has no partial conjugation, then
\Gamma
splits as
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{k}
(possibly with
k=1
), in such a way that every
\Gamma_{j}
is transvection-free, strongly reduced, and has at least two vertices. Proposition
5.3
thus follows from Proposition
8.1
.
∎

This completes the proof of the main theorem of
part

II
(
1.2
,
1.2
).

9
Amenable vertex groups and untransvectable vertices

9.1
Main result

The contents of this section is not needed for the main theorems of Part
II
, but will be used in Section
13
. In this section specifically, we will consider graph products where all vertex groups are amenable. In this case, more can be said without assuming that the graph has no transvection and no partial conjugation. Namely, given a subgroupoid
\mathcal{G}
over a standard probability space
X
equipped with an action-like cocycle
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to G
, we can still recognize subgroupoids that correspond to parabolic subgroups of untransvectable vertex type, in the following sense.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite simple graph. Recall that a vertex
v\in V\Gamma
is
untransvectable
if there does not exist any vertex
w\in V\Gamma
distinct from
v
and such that
\mathrm{lk}(v)\subseteq\mathrm{star}(w)
. A parabolic subgroup
P\subseteq G
is
of untransvectable vertex type
if it is conjugate to
G_{v}
for some untransvectable vertex
v
.

Given
\mathcal{G}
and
\rho
as above, and a measured subgroupoid
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
, we say that
(\mathcal{H},\rho)
is
of untransvectable vertex type
if there exist a conull Borel subset
X^{*}\subseteq X
, a partition
X^{*}=\sqcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
into at most countably many Borel subsets, and for every
i\in I
, a parabolic subgroup
P_{i}
of untransvectable vertex type such that for every
i\in I
, one has
\mathcal{H}_{|X_{i}}=\rho^{-1}(P_{i})_{|X_{i}}
.

We say that a finite simple graph
\Gamma
is
clique-reduced
if there do not exist distinct vertices
v,w\in V\Gamma
with
\mathrm{star}(v)=\mathrm{star}(w)
. Equivalently
\Gamma
is clique-reduced if and only if there does not exist any collapsible clique (in the sense of Definition
6.1
,
6.1
) on at least
2
vertices in
\Gamma
.

The main result of this section is the following proposition.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
G,H
be two graph products over clique-reduced finite simple graphs with countably infinite amenable vertex groups. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space, equipped with two action-like cocycles
\rho_{G}:\mathcal{G}\to G
and
\rho_{H}:\mathcal{G}\to H
. Let
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
be a measured subgroupoid.

Then, the pair
(\mathcal{V},\rho_{G})
is of untransvectable vertex type if and only if the pair
(\mathcal{V},\rho_{H})
is of untransvectable vertex type.

Remark 9.2
.

The assumption that the two graphs are clique-reduced is necessary. Otherwise, here is a counterexample. Let
G
be the graph product over the graph
\bar{\Gamma}
from Figure
11
,
fig.

11
, with vertex groups
\{G_{v}\}_{v}
isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Then
G
is also isomorphic to the graph product
H
over the graph
\Gamma
(from the same figure) with vertex groups isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
. We fix an isomorphism
\theta:H\to G
sending vertex groups inside vertex groups. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid with an action-like cocycle
\rho_{G}:\mathcal{G}\to G
, and let
\rho_{H}=\rho_{G}
(through the chosen isomorphism
H\to G
). If
v\in V\bar{\Gamma}
, then
(\rho_{G}^{-1}(G_{v}),\rho_{G})
is of untransvectable vertex type, while
(\rho_{G}^{-1}(G_{v}),\rho_{H})
is not.

Our motivation behind proving Proposition
9.1
is that it is a useful in the measure equivalence classification of right-angled Artin groups. In fact, as recorded in Corollary
9.4
below, it provides finer information than
[
HH22a
]
, which only dealt with right-angled Artin groups with finite outer automorphism groups. Among concrete applications, let us mention the following.

—

Corollary
9.4
below will be used in Section
10
to provide new concrete examples of right-angled Artin groups that can be distinguished from the viewpoint of measure equivalence.

—

In Part
III
of the present work, Proposition
9.1
will enable us to obtain quantitative estimates and solve the inverse problem in quantitative orbit equivalence for a large family of right-angled Artin groups.

—

In ongoing work of Huang and the second-named author (which has largely motivated the present section), the authors expect to use the results obtained here towards finer results in measure equivalence classification of the right-angled Artin groups.

To state our main corollary, we make the following definition.

Definition 9.3

(Untransvectable extension graph)
.

Let
G
be a graph product over a finite simple graph
\Gamma
. The
untransvectable extension graph
is the subgraph
\Gamma_{G}^{ue}\subseteq\Gamma_{G}^{e}
spanned by all vertices corresponding to parabolic subgroups of untransvectable vertex type.

Notice that
\Gamma_{G}^{ue}
is a
G
-invariant subgraph of
\Gamma_{G}^{e}
, for the
G
-action on
\Gamma_{G}^{e}
coming from the
G
-action by conjugation on itself. We warn the reader that
\Gamma_{G}^{ue}
might be disconnected, and might be empty.

We also refer to Definition
4.22
,
4.22
for the definition of an ME-witness.

Corollary 9.4
.

Let
G
and
H
be two graph products over finite simple graphs
\Gamma_{G},\Gamma_{H}
, with countably infinite amenable vertex groups.

Then
(\Gamma_{G}^{ue},\Gamma_{H}^{ue})
is an ME-witness for
G,H
. In particular, if
G
and
H
are measure equivalent, then
\Gamma_{G}^{ue}
and
\Gamma_{H}^{ue}
are isomorphic.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space, and let
\rho_{G}:\mathcal{G}\to G
and
\rho_{H}:\mathcal{G}\to H
be two action-like cocycles. Notice that
(\mathcal{G},\rho_{G})
is stably
\Gamma_{G}^{ue}
in the sense of Section
4.4
if and only if it is of untransvectable vertex type. Therefore, the first point of Definition
4.22
is exactly the contents of Proposition
9.1
,
9.1
. And the second point of Definition
4.22
is a consequence of Lemma
5.4
,
5.4
, which characterizes adjacency in the (untransvectable) extension graph with no reference to the action-like cocycle. Finally, the additional part of the corollary follows from Proposition
4.24
,
4.24
.
∎

In particular, Corollary
9.4
establishes the untransvectable extension graph as a new invariant in the measure equivalence classification of right-angled Artin groups. Notice that when
\Gamma_{G}
and
\Gamma_{H}
are transvection-free, then
\Gamma_{G}^{ue}=\Gamma_{G}^{e}
and
\Gamma_{H}^{ue}=\Gamma_{H}^{e}
, and Corollary
9.4
recovers
[
HH22a
, Theorem 2]
. Let us mention that, although we believe that the techniques from
[
HH22a
]
could have been extended to prove the results from the present section, we decided to use a different path, more in the spirit of the previous sections of the present work.

9.2
Detecting clique subgroups

We make the following definition.

Definition 9.5

(Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space. Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be a measured subgroupoid. We say that
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
if the following two properties hold.

(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})_{1}

The subgroupoid
\mathcal{C}
is amenable, of infinite type, and stably normal in
\mathcal{P}
.

(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})_{2}

If
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is a measured subgroupoid of
\mathcal{P}
such that
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})_{1}
, then
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is stably contained in
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
G
be a graph product of countably infinite amenable groups over a finite simple graph
\Gamma
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space, equipped with an action-like cocycle
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to G
. Let
P\subseteq G
be a product parabolic subgroup, and let
\mathcal{P}=\rho^{-1}(P)
.

Let
C\subseteq P
be the clique factor of
P
.

—

If
C=\{1\}
then no measured subgroupoid
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{P}
is such that
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
.

—

If
C
is non-trivial then a measured subgroupoid
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
is such that
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
if and only if
\mathcal{C}
is stably equal to
\rho^{-1}(C)
.

Proof.

We first assume that
C=\{1\}
, and assume towards a contradiction that there exists a measured subgroupoid
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
such that
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
. Then
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{cofact}})
(recall Definition
8.2
,
8.2
). So Lemma
8.3
,
8.3
applies and shows that
\mathcal{P}
is stably equal to
\rho^{-1}(Q)
for some cofactor
Q\subseteq P
. This is a contradiction to Lemma
4.14
,
4.14
using that
\rho
is action-like.

We now assume that
C\neq\{1\}
.

—

We first show that
\rho^{-1}(C)
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
.

The subgroup
C
is infinite and amenable because vertex groups are infinite and amenable. Let
\mathcal{C}=\rho^{-1}(C)
. Then
\mathcal{C}
is amenable because
C
is amenable and
\rho
has trivial kernel (Lemma
4.7
,
4.7
), of infinite type because
C
is infinite and
\rho
is action-like, and normal in
\mathcal{P}
because
C
is normal in
P
(Example
4.6
,
4.6
). So
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})_{1}
.

To check Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})_{2}
, let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be an amenable subgroupoid of infinite type which is stably normal in
\mathcal{P}
. We aim to prove that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is stably contained in
\mathcal{C}
. Without loss of generality, we will assume that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is normalized by
\mathcal{P}
. We write
P=C\times F_{1}\times\dots\times F_{k}
, where the subgroups
F_{j}
do not further split as a direct product. For every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, let
\rho_{j}:\mathcal{P}\to F_{j}
be the cocycle obtained by post-composing
\rho
by the projection
G\to F_{j}
. Up to a countable Borel partition of
X
, we will assume using Lemma
4.16
,
4.16
that
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\rho)
is tightly
A
-supported for some parabolic subgroup
A\subseteq P
.
We aim to prove that
A\subseteq C
. So assume by contradiction that this is not the case. Using Lemma
2.7
,
2.7
we can find
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
A\cap F_{j}\neq\{1\}
. In particular
(\rho_{j})_{|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
is nowhere trivial. Since
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is amenable, Lemma
6.11
,
6.11
and Remark
6.12
,
6.12
ensure that
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\rho_{j})
is
F_{j}
-elementary. Lemma
6.14
,
6.14
therefore implies that
(\mathcal{P},\rho_{j})
is also
F_{j}
-elementary. In particular
(\rho^{-1}(F_{j}),\rho_{j})
is
F_{j}
-elementary, which contradicts Lemma
6.10
,
lemma

6.10
(since
F_{j}
is not a clique). Therefore
A\cap F_{j}=\{1\}
for all
j
and thus
A\subseteq C
as wanted.

—

The stability of Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
under restriction and stabilization implies that any subgroupoid stably equal to
\rho^{-1}(C)
, satisfies itself Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
.

—

Now if
\mathcal{C}
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
, we showed in the first point that it is stably contained in
\rho^{-1}(C)
. By the maximality property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})_{2}
it is therefore stably equal to
\rho^{-1}(C)
.∎

9.3
Proof of Proposition
9.1

When all vertex groups are amenable, subgroupoids of isolated clique type are amenable, and therefore they do not satisfy Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{prod}})
from Definition
7.2
,
7.2
.

Lemma
7.4
,
7.4
thus reformulates as follows in this case.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
G
be a graph product of countably infinite amenable groups over a finite simple graph
\Gamma
.
Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space
X
, equipped with an action-like cocycle
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to G
. Let
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
be a measured subgroupoid.

Then
(\mathcal{P},\rho)
is of maximal product type and nowhere of isolated clique type if and only if
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{P})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{prod}})
. ∎

We now introduce the following variation over Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{vert}})
(Definition
7.12
).

Definition 9.8

(Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space
X
, and let
\mathcal{V}
be a measured subgroupoid of
\mathcal{G}
. We say that the pair
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{V})
satisfies
Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})
if the following two properties hold.

(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{1}

There exists a countable Borel partition
X^{*}=\sqcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
of a conull Borel subset
X^{*}\subseteq X
such that for every
i\in I
, there exist measured subgroupoids

\mathcal{G}_{|X_{i}}=\mathcal{F}_{0}\supseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{F}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}\supseteq\mathcal{F}_{2}\supseteq\dots\supseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\supseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}=\mathcal{V}_{|X_{i}}

of
\mathcal{G}_{|X_{i}}
such that

1.

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, the pair
(\mathcal{F}_{j-1},\mathcal{P}_{j})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{prod}})
(see Definition
7.2
,
7.2
);

2.

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, the pair
(\mathcal{P}_{j},\mathcal{F}_{j})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fact}})
(see Definition
8.4
,
8.4
);

3.

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
and every positive measure Borel subset
U\subseteq X_{i}
, there does not exist any subgroupoid
\mathcal{C}
of
(\mathcal{P}_{j})_{|U}
such that
((\mathcal{P}_{j})_{|U},\mathcal{C})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
(see Definition
9.5
,
9.5
);

4.

the pair
(\mathcal{P}_{n},\mathcal{C}_{n})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
.

(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{2}

For every measured subgroupoid
\mathcal{W}
of
\mathcal{G}
of infinite type which is stably contained in
\mathcal{V}
, and every measured subgroupoid
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
, if
\mathcal{W}
is stably normalized by
\mathcal{H}
, then
\mathcal{V}
is stably normalized by
\mathcal{H}
.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
G
be a graph product of countably infinite amenable groups over a clique-reduced finite simple graph
\Gamma
.
Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space
X
, equipped with an action-like cocycle
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to G
. Let
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
be a measured subgroupoid.

Then
(\mathcal{V},\rho)
is of untransvectable vertex type if and only if
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{V})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})
.

Proof.

We first assume that
(\mathcal{V},\rho)
is of untransvectable vertex type, and prove that
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{V})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})
.

Up to passing to a conull Borel subset of
X
, taking a countable partition, and conjugating the cocycle, we will assume that
\mathcal{V}=\rho^{-1}(G_{v})
, where
v\in V\Gamma
is untransvectable.

We start with
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{1}
. Let

G=F_{0}\supseteq P_{1}\supseteq F_{1}\supseteq\dots\supseteq P_{n}\supseteq C_{n}=G_{v}

be parabolic subgroups provided by Lemma
6.6
,
lemma

6.6
(here we write
F_{n}=C_{n}
). For every
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, let
\mathcal{P}_{j}=\rho^{-1}(P_{j})
and
\mathcal{F}_{j-1}=\rho^{-1}(F_{j-1})
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{n}=\rho^{-1}(C_{n})
. Since
P_{j}
is a maximal product parabolic subgroup of
F_{j-1}
and is not of isolated clique type, Lemma
7.4
,
7.4
ensures that
(\mathcal{F}_{j-1},\mathcal{P}_{j})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{prod}})
for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Since
P_{j}
has trivial clique factor for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, and
F_{j}
is a factor of
P_{j}
, Lemma
8.5
,
lemma

8.5
applies and shows that
(\mathcal{P}_{j},\mathcal{F}_{j})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fact}})
. Since
C_{n}
is the clique factor of
P_{n}
, Lemma
9.6
,
9.6
ensures that
(\mathcal{P}_{n},\mathcal{C}_{n})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
. On the other hand, for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, since
P_{j}
has trivial clique factor, Lemma
9.6
ensures that for every positive measure Borel subset
U\subseteq X
, there does not exist any measured subgroupoid
\mathcal{C}
of
(\mathcal{P}_{j})_{|U}
such that
((\mathcal{P}_{j})_{|U},\mathcal{C})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{clique}})
. This proves Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{1}
.

We now prove that
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{V})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{2}
. Let
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{H}
be as in
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{2}
. Since
\mathcal{W}
is of infinite type and stably contained in
\mathcal{V}
, up to a countable Borel partition of a conull Borel subset of
X
, we will assume that
(\mathcal{W},\rho)
is tightly
G_{v}
-supported. As
\mathcal{W}
is stably normalized by
\mathcal{H}
, Lemma
4.17
,
4.17
allows us to further assume that
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\rho^{-1}(G_{v}\times G_{v}^{\perp})
. But since
G_{v}
is normal in
G_{v}\times G_{v}^{\perp}
, it follows that
\mathcal{V}
is (stably) normalized by
\mathcal{H}
(Example
4.6
,
4.6
), as desired.

Conversely, let us assume that
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{V})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})
, and prove that
(\mathcal{V},\rho)
is of untransvectable vertex type.

Up to a countable partition, we can assume that there exist measured subgroupoids

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}_{0}\supseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{F}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}\supseteq\mathcal{F}_{2}\supseteq\dots\supseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\supseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}=\mathcal{V}

as in
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{1}
. Using Lemmas
8.5
,
9.6
and
9.7
, up to a further partition, we can assume that there exists a chain of parabolic subgroups

G=F_{0}\supseteq P_{1}\supseteq F_{1}\supseteq\dots\supseteq P_{n}\supseteq C_{n}

such that

1.

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, one has
\mathcal{P}_{j}=\rho^{-1}(P_{j})
, and
\mathcal{F}_{j-1}=\rho^{-1}(F_{j-1})
, and
\mathcal{C}_{n}=\rho^{-1}(C_{n})
, and

2.

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
P_{j}
is a maximal product parabolic subgroup of
F_{j-1}
and not of isolated clique type (Lemma
9.7
,
9.7
using the first assertion of
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{1}
),

3.

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, the clique factor of
P_{j}
is trivial (Lemma
9.6
, using the third assertion of
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{1}
), and
F_{j}
is a factor of
P_{j}
(Lemma
8.5
,
lemma

8.5
using the second assertion of
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{1}
), and

4.

C_{n}
is the clique factor of
P_{n}
(Lemma
9.6
,
9.6
using the fourth assertion of
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{1}
).

We now claim that
C_{n}
is conjugate to
G_{v}
for some
v\in V\Gamma
. This will be enough to conclude our proof, as Lemma
6.6
,
lemma

6.6
then implies that
v
is untransvectable.

We are thus left with proving the above claim. Assume that
C_{n}
is conjugate to
G_{\Upsilon}
, for a clique subgraph
\Upsilon\subseteq\Gamma
which contains at least two vertices. Up to conjugating the cocycle, we will assume without loss of generality that
C_{n}=G_{\Upsilon}
. Since
\Gamma
is clique-reduced, we can find a vertex
w\in V\Upsilon
such that
\mathrm{lk}(w)\nsubseteq\Upsilon\circ\Upsilon^{\perp}
. We now aim for a contradiction using Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{2}
. So let
\mathcal{W}=\rho^{-1}(G_{w})
, and let
\mathcal{H}=\rho^{-1}(G_{w}\times G_{w}^{\perp})
. Then
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, and
\mathcal{W}
is normalized by
\mathcal{H}
(Example
4.6
,
4.6
). Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})_{2}
implies that
\mathcal{V}
is stably normalized by
\mathcal{H}
. Since
(\mathcal{V},\rho)
is tightly
G_{\Upsilon}
-supported (Lemma
4.15
,
4.15
), Lemma
4.17
enables us to find a positive measure Borel subset
U\subseteq X
such that
\mathcal{H}_{|U}\subseteq\rho^{-1}(G_{\Upsilon}\times G_{\Upsilon}^{\perp})
. It follows from Lemma
4.14
,
4.14
that
G_{w}\times G_{w}^{\perp}\subseteq G_{\Upsilon}\times G_{\Upsilon}^{\perp}
, which contradicts our choice of
w
. This contradiction completes our proof.
∎

We are now in position to complete the proof of the main proposition of this section.

Proof of Proposition
9.1
.

Assume that
(\mathcal{V},\rho_{G})
is of untransvectable vertex type. By Lemma
9.9
applied to the cocycle
\rho_{G}
, the pair
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{V})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{vert}})
. And Lemma
9.9
applied to the cocycle
\rho_{H}
then proves that
(\mathcal{V},\rho_{H})
is of untransvectable vertex type. By symmetry the other implication also holds.
∎

10
Examples

To conclude this part, we provide some examples illustrating the different cases treated in the past sections. All the graph products presented in this section are in fact right-angled Artin groups (though they are sometimes viewed as graph products with non-cyclic vertex groups). So our work provides new cases of the measure equivalence classification of right-angled Artin groups, beyond those treated in
[
HH22a
]
.
On the other hand, some cases remain open, such as right-angled Artin groups defined over trees.

Example 10.1
.

The following example is an illustration of
1.2
.

The two graph products defined in
fig.

18
are not measure equivalent.

Indeed, the graph (namely the pentagon) is transvection-free and has no partial conjugation. Therefore
1.2
applies: if
G
and
H
were measure equivalent, then we would have a graph isomorphism sending the vertex group
F_{2}
to an orbit equivalent vertex group in
H
. Such a vertex group does not exist in
H
. Therefore
G
and
H
cannot be measure equivalent.

Figure 18
:
Groups from
10.1

Example 10.2
.

The following example is an illustration of Corollary
5.7
.

Let
G
and
H
be as in
fig.

19
(the two leftmost graphs). If
G
and
H
were measure equivalent, then we would have a graph homomorphism
\sigma:\Gamma_{G}\rightarrow\Gamma_{H}
such that
G_{v}
is measure equivalent to
H_{\sigma(v)}
for all
v\in V\Gamma_{G}
. In particular
\sigma
must send
blue
vertices to
blue
vertices. In particular, by looking at the restriction of
\sigma
to the leftmost pentagon in
\Gamma_{G}
(all of whose vertices are
blue
), there would exist a graph homomorphism from a pentagon to a segment of length
3
, which is not the case. Therefore
G
and
H
are not measure equivalent.

Figure 19
:
Graphs and groups from
10.2
and
10.3

Remark 10.3
.

We do not know however whether or not the groups
H
and
G^{\prime}
are measure equivalent. There do exist graph homomorphisms
\Gamma_{G}\to\Gamma_{H}
and
\Gamma_{H}\to\Gamma_{G}
, such that a vertex and its image always carry measure equivalent vertex groups, so Corollary
5.7
does not provide any obstruction. In fact, if the
brown
vertex group was isomorphic to
F_{2}
, then
G^{\prime}
would be isomorphic to an index
2
subgroup of
H
(whence measure equivalent to
H
). However, with the
brown
vertex group isomorphic to
F_{3}
, we have no reason to expect that the groups
G^{\prime}
and
H
should be measure equivalent.

Example 10.4
.

The following example is an illustration of
9.1
.

Let
G
and
H
be the two groups defined in the left half of
fig.

20
,
fig.

20
. Then
G
and
H
are not measure equivalent.

Indeed, first remark that in
fig.

20
all untransvectable vertices are drawn in
orange
. Second, note that
H
can be rewritten as a right-angled Artin group over the graph drawn at the top of the right half of
fig.

20
(in particular, with this decomposition all vertex groups are now amenable). In the latter graph, all vertices are transvectable.
If
G
and
H
were measure equivalent then we could apply
9.1
,
9.1
and get the existence of an
untransvectable
vertex in the rightmost graph defining
H
. Hence
G
and
H
can not be measure equivalent. For the same reason, the groups
H
and
H^{\prime}
are not measure equivalent.

In fact, as a consequence of Corollary
9.4
, we can also prove that
G
and
H^{\prime}
are not measure equivalent. Indeed, the untransvectable extension graph
\Gamma_{H^{\prime}}^{e}
(see Definition
9.3
) is totally disconnected: its vertices represent the conjugates of the leftmost
orange
vertex group and of the rightmost
orange
vertex group, and no two distinct conjugates of these vertex groups commute. On the other hand
\Gamma_{G}^{e}
contains an edge, because all
orange
vertex groups are untransvectable.

One could provide many variations over the above example. But many cases remain open: the most basic ones is that our methods do not allow to decide whether or not right-angled Artin groups defined over trees of diameter at least
3
are measure equivalent or not.

:
Groups from
10.4

Part III
Quantitative aspects and the inverse problem for RAAGs

The goal of the present part is to obtain a quantitative version of the previous classification theorem (
1.2
,
1.2
) and solve the inverse problem in quantitative measure equivalence for a large family of right-angled Artin groups. We refer to
section

3.1.3
,
section

3.1.3
for the definition of quantitative orbit equivalence.

In
section

11
we prove that when two graph products are defined over the same graph, the integrability at the level of the vertex groups can be lifted at the level of the graph products themselves (
1.10
). This actually does not need any assumption on the defining graph and relies on a previous result of Huang and the second named author
[
HH22a
]
, generalizing an argument of Gaboriau
[
Gab05
,
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{ME}}\mathbf{6}
]
. In
section

12
we show a generalized version of
1.11
, proving for a large family of graph products that the integrability at the level of the graph products passes at the level of the vertex groups.

The inverse problem is finally solved in
section

13
.

11
From the factors to the graph product

We prove here
1.10
which we recall below – in the introduction, we used the language of integrable orbit equivalence couplings instead of pairings, but these are equivalent, see Section
3.1.3
.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
G
and
H
be two graph products over a finite simple graph
\Gamma
, with finitely generated vertex groups
(G_{v})_{v\in V\Gamma}
and
(H_{v})_{v\in V\Gamma}
, respectively.

Let
\varphi,\psi:[1,+\infty[\rightarrow[1,+\infty[
be two non-decreasing functions. Assume that for every
v\in V\Gamma
, there exists a
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable orbit equivalence pairing from
G_{v}
to
H_{v}
.

Then there exists a
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable orbit equivalence pairing from

G
to
H
.

This theorem is a quantitative version of
[
HH22a
, Proposition 4.2]
. Our proof is actually based on the pairing built in
[
HH22a
]
to prove the latter proposition. In order to
keep this article self contained we recall the construction below.

Construction of the pairing

From now on we fix an enumeration
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
of the vertices of
\Gamma
.

For all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, let
(Y_{i},\nu_{i})
be an orbit equivalence pairing between
G_{v_{i}}
and
H_{v_{i}}
. Consider also a free measure-preserving action of
G
on a standard probability space
Z
. Let
X=Z\times Y_{1}\times\cdots\times Y_{n}
and endow
X
with the product probability measure denoted by
\mu
. An element
x
of
X
is denoted by
x=(z,y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
where
z\in Z
and
y_{i}\in Y_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. We also denote by
r_{i}:G\to G_{v_{i}}
the retraction which is the identity on
G_{v_{i}}
and sends all other factors to
\{1\}
. We can thus define a free measure-preserving
G
-action on
X
, where the action on each
Y_{i}
is through
r_{i}
, that is to say:

g(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})=\left(gz,r_{1}(g)y_{1},\ldots,r_{n}(g)y_{n}\right).

(11.1)

To define the action of
H
on
X
we first define the action of
H_{v_{i}}
on

X
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. So let
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and denote by
c_{i}:G_{v_{i}}\times Y_{i}\rightarrow H_{v_{i}}
and
c^{\prime}_{i}:H_{v_{i}}\times Y_{i}\rightarrow G_{v_{i}}
the two orbit equivalence cocycles associated to the pairing between
G_{v_{i}}
and
H_{v_{i}}
. Recall that
c_{i}(g,y_{i})
is defined for a.e.
y_{i}\in Y_{i}
, as the unique
h\in H_{v_{i}}
such that
gy_{i}=hy_{i}
. Similarly
c^{\prime}_{i}(h,y_{i})
is the unique
g\in G_{v_{i}}
such that
gy_{i}=hy_{i}
. Hence, for
h\in H_{v_{i}}
we define for a.e.
(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\in X
,

h(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}):=c^{\prime}_{i}(h,y_{i})(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}).

This leads to a well defined free action of
H_{v_{i}}
on
X
.

To obtain an action of the entire group
H
, we now show that if
h_{i},h_{j}
belong to adjacent vertex groups
H_{v_{i}}
,
H_{v_{j}}
respectively, then
h_{i}\big(h_{j}(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\big)=h_{j}\big(h_{i}(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\big)
.

First remark that, by definition of the retractions, when
g
belongs to some vertex group
G_{v_{l}}
the above
eq.

11.1
becomes

g(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})=\left(gz,\,y_{1},\,\ldots,\,y_{l-1},\,gy_{l},\,y_{l+1},\,\ldots,\,y_{n}\right).

(11.2)

Finally, since for all
l
the cocycle
c^{\prime}_{l}
goes from
H_{v_{l}}\times Y_{l}
to
G_{v_{l}}
and since
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
are adjacent in
\Gamma
, we thus get that
c^{\prime}_{i}(h_{i},y_{i})
and
c^{\prime}_{j}(h_{j},y_{j})
belong to adjacent vertex groups. Therefore, using
eq.

11.2
for the second and the fourth equalities, we get

\displaystyle h_{i}\Big(h_{j}(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle=h_{i}\Big(c^{\prime}_{j}(h_{j},y_{j})(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\Big),

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{i}(h_{i},y_{i})\Big(c^{\prime}_{j}(h_{j},y_{j})(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\Big),

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{j}(h_{j},y_{j})\Big(c^{\prime}_{i}(h_{i},y_{i})(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\Big),

\displaystyle=h_{j}\Big(c^{\prime}_{i}(h_{i},y_{i})(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\Big),

\displaystyle=h_{j}\Big(h_{i}(z,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\Big).

The action defined at the level of the vertex groups thus extends at the level of the entire graph product
H
. The fact that this
H
-action is free follows from the freeness of the
G
-action on
Z
and a normal form argument.

We can now use this construction to prove our result.

Proof of
1.10
.

Keeping all notations from above, let
X=Z\times Y_{1}\times\cdots\times Y_{n}
be the
pairing defined in the last paragraph, where for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
we choose
Y_{i}
to be a
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable orbit equivalence pairing from
G_{v_{i}}
to
H_{v_{i}}
. Denote by
c:G\times X\rightarrow H
and
c^{\prime}:H\times X\rightarrow G
the two cocycles associated to the pairing
(X,\mu)
. Finally recall that an element
x
of
X
is denoted by
x=(z,y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
where
z\in Z
and
y_{i}\in Y_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

For any
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
denote by
S^{i}_{G}
and
S^{i}_{H}

finite generating sets of

G_{v_{i}}
and
H_{v_{i}}
respectively. Then
S_{G}:=\sqcup^{n}_{i=1}S^{i}_{G}
generates
G
and
S_{H}:=\sqcup^{n}_{i=1}S^{i}_{H}
generates
H
. Therefore, to prove that this pairing is
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable we only need to show that for all
s\in S_{G}
and all
s^{\prime}\in S_{H}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{x\in X}\varphi\left(\big|c(s,x)\big|_{S_{H}}\right)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle<+\infty,

(11.3)

\displaystyle\int_{x\in X}\psi\left(\big|c^{\prime}(s^{\prime},x)\big|_{S_{G}}\right)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle<+\infty.

(11.4)

\varphi
-integrability

Consider
s\in S_{G}
and let
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
be such that
s\in S^{i}_{G}
. For a.e.
x\in X
, using first the definition of the action of
H
on
X
and then using that
c^{\prime}_{i}\big(c_{i}(s,y_{i}),y_{i}\big)=s
, we obtain

c_{i}(s,y_{i})x=c^{\prime}_{i}\big(c_{i}(s,y_{i}),y_{i}\big)x=sx,

and thus
c(s,x)=c_{i}(s,y_{i})
. Therefore, by choice of
S_{H}
we get that

|c(s,x)|_{S_{H}}=|c_{i}(s,y_{i})|_{S_{H}}=|c_{i}(s,y_{i})|_{S^{i}_{H}}.

Finally, using that all spaces
Z
and
Y_{j}
have measure
1
and that
c_{i}
is
\varphi
-integrable on
Y_{i}
, we thus obtain that
eq.

11.3
is verified.

\psi
-integrability
Now consider
s^{\prime}\in S_{H}
and let
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
be such that
s^{\prime}\in S^{i}_{H}
. Then by definition of the
H
-action on
X
, we have
s^{\prime}x=c^{\prime}_{i}\left(s^{\prime},y_{i}\right)x
for a.e.
x\in X
. Therefore
c^{\prime}(s^{\prime},x)=c^{\prime}_{i}(s^{\prime},y_{i})
for a.e.
x\in X
, and since the latter element belongs to
G_{v_{i}}
we thus obtain

\left|c^{\prime}\big(s^{\prime},x\big)\right|_{S_{G}}=\left|c^{\prime}_{i}\left(s^{\prime},y_{i}\right)\right|_{S_{G}}=\left|c^{\prime}_{i}\left(s^{\prime},y_{i}\right)\right|_{S^{i}_{G}}.

But by assumption,
c^{\prime}_{i}
is
\psi
-integrable, thus
eq.

11.4
is verified and hence the pairing constructed above is
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable.
∎

12
From the graph product to the factors

The goal of this section is to prove
1.11
,
1.11
, which states that the quantification at the level of the graph products passes at the level of the vertex groups.

12.1
The general statement and applications

12.1.1
Main result

What we actually show here is the following more general version of the latter theorem. We refer to
5.1
,
5.1
for the definition of the Vertex Recognition Property and to
section

2.2.3
,
section

2.2.3
for the definition of an untransvectable vertex.

Theorem 12.1
.

Let
\varphi,\psi:[1,+\infty[\rightarrow[1,+\infty[
be two increasing unbounded functions and let
G
and
H
be two graph products defined over
\Gamma_{G},\Gamma_{H}
respectively, with infinite finitely generated vertex groups. Assume that there exists a
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
G
to
H
.

1.

If for all
v\in V\Gamma_{G}
and all
w\in V\Gamma_{H}
the vertex groups
G_{v}
and
H_{w}
are amenable then for all untransvectable vertices
v\in V\Gamma_{G}
, their exist
\sigma(v)\in V\Gamma_{H}
and a
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
G_{v}
to
H_{\sigma(v)}
.

2.

If
G
and
H
both belong to a same class of groups
\mathsf{C}
satisfying the Vertex Recognition Property, then there exist a graph homomorphism
\sigma:\Gamma_{G}\rightarrow\Gamma_{H}
and for all
v\in V\Gamma_{G}
a
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
G_{v}
to
H_{\sigma(v)}
.

3.

If
\Gamma_{G}
and
\Gamma_{H}
are transvection-free with no partial conjugation, then there exists a graph isomorphism
\sigma:\Gamma_{G}\to\Gamma_{H}
and for all
v\in V\Gamma_{G}
a
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
G_{v}
to
H_{\sigma(v)}
.

The second assertion applies in particular whenever
\Gamma_{G}
and
\Gamma_{H}
are transvection-free and strongly reduced (Proposition
7.1
,
7.1
).

The three conclusions of the above theorem provide quantitative versions of three situations already encountered in Part
II
of this work. Namely,

—

the first part is a quantitative version of the work from Section
9
;

—

the second part is a version of Corollary
5.7
,
5.7
;

—

the third part (which is exactly Theorem
1.11
,
1.11
from the introduction), is a quantitative version of Theorem
1.2
.

We also refer to Section
10
for illustrations of these various situations.

Recall also that when
G
is a right-angled Artin group, asking
\Gamma_{G}
to be transvection-free with no partial conjugation is equivalent to requiring
\mathrm{Out}(G)
to be finite. So in particular the first and second assertions of our theorem apply to a larger family of right-angled Artin groups than the ones with finite outer automorphism group.

Finally, we refer to Remark
1.12
for a discussion on the asymmetry between Theorem
1.10
(stated in terms of orbit equivalence) and Theorem
12.1
(stated in terms of measure equivalence).

12.1.2
Obstructions in the non-amenable world

As explained in the introduction, quantitative measure equivalence responds well to geometry, which sometimes provides us with upper bounds to the possible quantifications between two given groups. So far, most of the known obstructions were in the amenable world, our
12.1
above allows to extend them to a large family of non-amenable groups. We recall below some of the known geometric obstructions and detail the case of the isoperimetric profile that will be used in the next section.

Among the previously known obstructions were the results of Bowen and Austin in
[
Aus16
]
. Indeed, Bowen proved that growth is stable under integrable measure equivalence. Austin moreover showed that two
L^{1}
-measure equivalent groups of polynomial growth have bi-Lipschitz asymptotic cones. This implies for example that
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
and the Heisenberg group
H_{3}
, although having the same growth rate, cannot be
L^{1}
-measure equivalent. Another obstruction relies on the notion of
isoperimetric profile
. Let
S_{G}
be a finite generating set of
G
and recall that the
S_{G}
-boundary
of a finite subset
A
of
G
is defined as
\partial_{S_{G}}A:=\{g\in A\ :\ \exists s\in S_{G},\ gs\notin A\}
. The
isoperimetric profile
of a finitely generated group
G
, with respect to a finite generating set
S
is the function
I_{G}:\mathbb{N}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

I_{G}(n):=\sup_{A\subseteq G,\ 0<|A|\leq n}\frac{|A|}{|\partial_{S_{G}}A|}.

Note that (by the Følner criterion) a group is amenable if and only if its isoperimetric profile is unbounded.

Delabie, Koivisto, Le Maître and Tessera
[
DKLMT22
]
showed the monotonicity under quantitative measure equivalence of the isoperimetric profile and more precisely of its
asymptotic behaviour
. More formally, if
f
and
g
are two real functions we denote
f\preccurlyeq g
if there exists some constant
C>0
such that
f(x)=\mathcal{O}\big(g(Cx)\big)
as
x
tends to infinity. We write
f\simeq g
if
f\preccurlyeq g
and
g\preccurlyeq f
.

Theorem 12.2

(
[
DKLMT22
, Th.1]
)
.

Let
G
and
H
be two finitely generated groups admitting a

(\varphi,L^{0})
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
G
to
H
. If both
\varphi
and
t/\varphi(t)
are non-decreasing then
\varphi\circ I_{H}\preccurlyeq I_{G}
.

As mentioned above, when
G
and
H
are non-amenable groups, both their isoperimetric profiles are bounded. Therefore, the latter theorem does not provide any obstruction for non-amenable groups. Our
12.1
, however, does: it allows us to extend the condition on the profile to the world of graph products as in the example below.

Example 12.3
.

Let
G
and
H
be the graph products defined in
fig.

21
.

By
[
DKLMT22
, Theorem 1.9]
, for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists an
(L^{1/2-\varepsilon},L^{0})
-integrable orbit equivalence pairing from
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
to
\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore by
1.10
, the groups
G
and
H
are
(L^{1/2-\varepsilon},L^{0})
-integrably orbit equivalent for all
\varepsilon>0
.

Now assume that we have a
(\varphi,L^{0})
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
G
to
H
. The
orange
vertex on the left is untransvectable, therefore
12.1
provides us with a vertex group in
G
that is
(\varphi,L^{0})
-integrably measure equivalent to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, namely a
(\varphi,L^{0})
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
to
\mathbb{Z}
.

Recall that
I_{\mathbb{Z}}(x)\simeq x
and
I_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}(x)\simeq\sqrt{x}
.
Applying
12.2
to the latter coupling gives that the integrability
\varphi
cannot be better than
L^{1/2}
.

Figure 21
:
Graph products of
12.3

The next section contains the final tools needed to prove the theorem.

12.2
Integrability at the vertex level

The strategy of the proof of Theorem
12.1
is the following.
In the three cases we will have, inside the coupling
\Omega
, a subcoupling between a vertex group of
G
and a vertex group of
H
. It will be obtained using the results from
part

II
and
lemma

12.4
below.

In the first case, we will be able to obtain such a coupling for all untransvectable vertices using
section

9
.
When working with the second case, namely the Vertex Recognition Property, we use
5.7
instead. And the third case relies on
5.10
. The final tool will be
12.5
, which provides a way to obtain the wanted integrability of the subcouplings between vertex groups.

We first start by rewriting
lemma

3.3
in the graph product framework and with the groupoid theoretic vocabulary, as explained in Remark
4.4
. We refer to
section

4
for background on measured groupoids.

Lemma 12.4

(Restating
lemma

3.3
)
.

Let
G
and
H
be two graph products of countably infinite vertex groups, defined over finite simple graphs
\Gamma_{G},\Gamma_{H}
.
Let
(\Omega,m,X_{G},X_{H})
be a measure equivalence coupling from
G
to
H
, chosen so that
X_{G}\cap X_{H}
has positive measure. Let
\mathcal{G}
be the associated measured groupoid over
X_{G}\cap X_{H}
and
\rho_{G}
(resp.
\rho_{H}
) the corresponding cocycles. Let
v\in V\Gamma_{G}
and
w\in V\Gamma_{H}
. Assume that there exists a positive measure Borel subset
U\subseteq X_{G}\cap X_{H}
such that

\rho_{G}^{-1}(G_{v})_{|U}=\rho_{H}^{-1}(H_{w})_{|U}.

Then there exist a
(G_{v}\times H_{w})
-invariant Borel subspace
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
, and Borel fundamental domains
X_{v},X_{w}
for the actions of
G_{v},H_{w}
on
\Omega^{\prime}
, such that

1.

(\Omega^{\prime},m,X_{v},X_{w})
is a measure equivalence coupling from
G_{v}
to
H_{w}
, and

2.

X_{v},X_{w}
are contained in fundamental domains for the respective actions of
G,H
on
\Omega
.

The following lemma, contrarily to the previous one, relies on the graph product structure since it makes use of the normal form.

Lemma 12.5
.

Let
G
and
H
be two graph products over finite simple graphs
\Gamma_{G},\Gamma_{H}
, and let
P_{G}\subseteq G
and
P_{H}\subseteq H
be two respective parabolic subgroups.
Let
(\Omega,m,X_{G},X_{H})
be a
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
G
to
H
. If there exist

—

a
(P_{G}\times P_{H})
-invariant Borel subset
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
, such that
(\Omega^{\prime},m,X_{P_{G}},X_{P_{H}})
is a measure equivalence coupling from
P_{G}
to
P_{H}
;

—

and
X^{\prime}_{G}
,
X^{\prime}_{H}
fundamental domains for the
G
- and
H
-actions respectively, such that
X_{P_{G}}\subseteq X^{\prime}_{G}
and
X_{P_{H}}\subseteq X^{\prime}_{H}
;

then
P_{G}
and
P_{H}
are
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrably measure equivalent over
\Omega^{\prime}
(for suitable fundamental domains).

Recall that when
(\Omega,m,X_{G},X_{H})
is a measure equivalence coupling from
G
to
H
, we have an induced action of
G
on
X_{H}
and an induced action of
H
on
X_{G}
; both actions are denoted by “
\cdot
” (see
section

3.1.2
and
fig.

1
,
fig.

1
). Recall also that given a fundamental domain
X_{H}
one can identify
\Omega/H
with
X_{H}
and – through this identification – changing the fundamental domain amounts to conjugating the cocycles and vice versa (see
3.1
,
3.1
).

Proof.

Up to replacing
\Omega^{\prime}
and the fundamental domains by translates, we can (and will) assume without loss of generality that
P_{G}=G_{\Upsilon}
and
P_{H}=H_{\Lambda}
for some induced subgraphs
\Upsilon\subseteq\Gamma_{G}
and
\Lambda\subseteq\Gamma_{H}
.

We will prove that
\Omega^{\prime}
is
(\varphi,L^{0})
-integrable (and thus exhibit an appropriate fundamental domain). By symmetry (i.e. interchanging the roles of
G
and
H
), this will be enough to derive that
\Omega^{\prime}
is also
(L^{0},\psi)
-integrable (again, for an appropriate fundamental domain), and the lemma will follow.

If
x
belongs to
X_{H}
we will denote by
x^{\prime}
the unique element of
X^{\prime}_{H}
in the same
H
-orbit as
x
.
Denote by
c
and
c^{\prime}
the measure equivalence cocycles associated to the fundamental domains
X_{H}
and
X^{\prime}_{H}
respectively, namely

\displaystyle c:G\times X_{H}

\displaystyle\rightarrow H,

\displaystyle c^{\prime}:G\times X^{\prime}_{H}

\displaystyle\rightarrow H.

By assumption,
c
is
\varphi
-integrable and since
X_{P_{H}}\subseteq X^{\prime}_{H}
we have
c^{\prime}(P_{G}\times X_{P_{H}})\subseteq P_{H}
. Moreover, since
X^{\prime}_{H}
and
X_{H}
are two fundamental domains for the same
H
-action, the above two cocycles are cohomologous. That is to say, there exists
\alpha:X_{H}\rightarrow H
such that
c^{\prime}(g,x^{\prime})=\alpha(g\cdot x)c(g,x)\alpha(x)^{-1}
for all
g\in G
and a.e.
x\in X_{H}
.

Now let
\vartheta:H\to H
be defined by letting
\vartheta(h)
be the element with the smallest word length such that
P_{H}\vartheta(h)=P_{H}h
. Equivalently
\vartheta(h)
is the unique representative of the coset
P_{H}h
whose head (see
section

2.1
for the definition) contains no element in
P_{H}
. By definition
\vartheta
is constant on all right cosets of
P_{H}
. Now let

X^{\prime\prime}_{H}:=\{\vartheta\big(\alpha(x)\big)\cdot x\ :\ x\in X_{H}\}.

As above, if
x
belongs to
X_{H}
we denote by
x^{\prime\prime}
the element of
X^{\prime\prime}_{H}
corresponding to the same
H
-orbit. We denote by
X^{\prime\prime}_{P_{H}}
the image of
X_{P_{H}}
under the identification
x^{\prime}\mapsto x^{\prime\prime}
. Let us prove that
X^{\prime\prime}_{P_{H}}
is the appropriate fundamental domain.

For all
g\in P_{G}
and a.e.
x\in X_{H}
we define

c^{\prime\prime}_{1}(g,x^{\prime\prime})=\vartheta\left(\alpha(g\cdot x)\right)c(g,x)\vartheta\big(\alpha(x)\big)^{-1}.

Remark that by definition of
\vartheta
this cocycle
c^{\prime\prime}_{1}
is
P_{H}
-cohomologous to
c^{\prime}
(through the identification
x^{\prime}\mapsto x^{\prime\prime}
). In particular, its restriction to
P_{G}\times X^{\prime\prime}_{P_{H}}
takes its values in
P_{H}
and also defines a measure equivalence cocycle on
\Omega^{\prime}
(namely, the measure equivalence cocycle associated to the fundamental domain
X^{\prime\prime}_{P_{H}}
).

Now, for all
v\in V\Gamma_{H}
let
S_{H_{v}}
be a finite generating set of
H_{v}
and recall that
S_{H}=\cup_{v\in V\Gamma_{H}}S_{H_{v}}
generates
H
. We also let
S_{P_{H}}:=\cup_{v\in V\Lambda}S_{H_{v}}
, where
\Lambda\subseteq\Gamma_{H}
is the type of
P_{H}
– notice that
S_{P_{H}}
is a finite generating set of
P_{H}
. Our definition of
\vartheta
, together with
2.2
,
2.2
imply that for a.e.
x^{\prime\prime}\in X^{\prime\prime}_{P_{H}}
we have

\left|c^{\prime\prime}_{1}(g,x^{\prime\prime})\right|_{S_{P_{H}}}=\left|c^{\prime\prime}_{1}(g,x^{\prime\prime})\right|_{S_{H}}\leq|c(g,x)|_{S_{H}}.

Hence
c^{\prime\prime}_{1}
is a
\varphi
-integrable cocycle since
c
is itself
\varphi
-integrable, as wanted.
∎

12.3
Proof of the main theorem

We now turn to the proof of
12.1
.

Proof of
12.1
.

Let
(\Omega,m,X_{G},X_{H})
be a
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
G
to
H
, and let
c_{1}:G\times X_{H}\to H
and
c_{2}:H\times X_{G}\rightarrow G
be the two associated measure equivalence cocycles. Up to replacing
X_{H}
by
hX_{H}
for some
h\in H
, we can (and will) assume that
\mu(X_{G}\cap X_{H})>0
– notice indeed that this changes the measure equivalence cocycle from
G
to
H
by a conjugation by
h
, and therefore does not affect its integrability. Let
X=X_{G}\cap X_{H}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be the measured groupoid over
X
given by the restriction of the
G
- and
H
-actions on
X_{H}
and
X_{G}
respectively (see
4.2
,
4.2
). It is naturally equipped with two cocycles
\rho_{G}:\mathcal{G}\to G
and
\rho_{H}:\mathcal{G}\to H
, which are action-like (Lemma
4.13
,
4.13
). We now distinguish three different cases corresponding to the three different sets of hypotheses in our theorem.

Case 1

If for all
v\in V\Gamma_{G}
and all
w\in V\Gamma_{H}
the vertex groups
G_{v}
and
H_{w}
are amenable then
9.1
,
9.1
ensures that for every untransvectable vertex
v\in V\Gamma_{G}
there exist
\sigma(v)\in V\Gamma_{H}
,
h\in H
and a Borel subset
U\subseteq X_{G}\cap X_{H}
of positive measure such that

\rho^{-1}_{G}(G_{v})_{|U}=\rho^{-1}_{H}(hH_{\sigma(v)}h^{-1})_{|U}.

Considering now
H^{\prime}
the graph product over
\Gamma_{H}
with vertex groups
\{hH_{v}h^{-1}\}_{v\in V\Gamma_{H}}
, we can apply
lemma

12.4
to
G
and
H^{\prime}
and obtain a measure equivalence coupling
\Omega^{\prime}
from
G_{v}
to
hH_{\sigma(v)}h^{-1}
verifying the hypotheses of
12.5
. The latter lemma gives the
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrability of
\Omega^{\prime}
, for well-chosen fundamental domains.

Case 2

If
G
and
H
both belong to a class of groups
\mathsf{C}
having the Vertex Recognition Property then the proof of
5.7
,
5.7
ensures that there exists a graph homomorphism
\sigma:\Gamma_{G}\rightarrow\Gamma_{H}
such that
for all

v\in V\Gamma_{G}
, the groups
G_{v}
and
H_{\sigma(v)}
verify the hypothesis of
lemma

12.4
. And therefore, as above there exists a
(\varphi,\psi)
-integrable coupling from
G_{v}
to
H_{\sigma(v)}
.

Case 3

If we assume that
\Gamma_{G}
and
\Gamma_{H}
are transvection-free graphs with no partial conjugation, then
5.10
and
5.11

ensure that there exist a graph isomorphism
\sigma:\Gamma_{G}\rightarrow\Gamma_{H}
, and for all
v\in V\Gamma_{G}
a Borel subset
\Omega_{v}\subseteq\Omega
which is a measure equivalence coupling from
G_{v}
to
H_{\sigma(v)}
and satisfy the hypotheses of
12.5
. The latter result then gives the desired integrability.∎

13
The inverse problem for right-angled Artin groups

The goal of this section is to prove
1.13
,
1.13
which answers the inverse problem for right-angled Artin groups, namely: given a right-angled Artin group
H
and a quantification
\varphi
, can we find a group
G
and a measure equivalence coupling which is
(\varphi,L^{0})
-integrable from
G
to
H
?

As discussed in the introduction (see
section

1.2.3
,
section

1.2.3
), we also ask this coupling to be optimal (or close to be). The optimality here comes from the
geometry
of the involved groups, more precisely from the obstruction given by the isoperimetric profile of the vertex groups (see
section

12.1.2
).

13.1
Main theorem

The following restates Theorem
1.13
from the introduction.

Theorem 1.13
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite simple graph that contains an untransvectable vertex
and let
\rho
be a non-decreasing map such that
x\mapsto x/\rho(x)
is non-decreasing. If
H
is a right-angled Artin group over
\Gamma
then there exists a group
G
and an orbit equivalence pairing from
G
to
H
which is a
(\varphi_{\varepsilon},L^{0})
-integrable for all
\varepsilon>0
, where

\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x):=\frac{\rho\circ\log(x)}{\left(\log\circ\rho\circ\log(x)\right)^{1+\varepsilon}}.

Moreover, this coupling is optimal up to a log factor in the following sense: if there exists a
(\varphi,L^{0})
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
G
to
H
, then
\varphi\preccurlyeq\rho\circ\log
.

Discussion on the hypotheses

First remark that the hypotheses on
\rho
lead us to deal with quantifications having sub-logarithmic growth. On the other hand, Huang and the second-named author showed a superrigidity phenomenon under
(L^{1},L^{0})
-measure equivalence
[
HH23
, Theorem 1]
. More precisely, they showed that if
G
is a right-angled Artin group with finite outer automorphism group and
H
is any countable group with bounded torsion such that there exists an
(L^{1},L^{0})
-measure equivalence coupling from
H
to
G
, then
H
is finitely generated and quasi-isometric to
G
. In some cases this implies that
G
and
H
are commensurable
[
Hua18
]
. We thus have here two completely different behaviors: a lot of flexibility for sub-logarithmic quantification versus superrigidity under
L^{1}
-integrability.

Second, we mention that one could extend the above theorem when
H
is a graph product over
\Gamma
of non-trivial finitely generated free abelian groups up to adding some assumptions on the map
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
(concavity) as in
[
Esc22
, Corollary 1.8]
.

On the other hand, we do not know whether the theorem holds when
H
is a non-abelian free group, and more generally we cannot treat the case where
H
splits as a graph product of non-abelian free groups.

13.2
Proof of
1.13

The proof relies on two main tools: the resolution of the above inverse problem for
H=\mathbb{Z}
(or equivalently, when
\Gamma
is reduced to one vertex) already established in
[
Esc22
]
, and the results specific to graph products developed in the present article, namely
1.10
and
12.1
(
1.10
and
12.1
).

We start by recalling the known results for the case of
\Gamma
reduced to one vertex and then turn to our proof of the above
1.13
.

Background on the inverse problem

When
H=\mathbb{Z}
(or equivalently, when
\Gamma
is reduced to one vertex) building couplings with prescribed integrability with
H
has been done by the first named author in
[
Esc22
, Theorem 1.7]
, which we recall below. The optimality is obtained in this case using the obstruction given in
12.2
, which relies on the isoperimetric profile (see
section

12.1.2
for the definition of the profile
I_{G}
).

Theorem 13.1

(Escalier
[
Esc22
]
)
.

For all non-decreasing maps
\rho
such that
x\mapsto x/\rho(x)
is non-decreasing, there exists a group
\Delta
such that

—

I_{\Delta}\simeq{\rho}\circ\log
;

—

There exists an orbit equivalence pairing that is
(\varphi_{\varepsilon},\exp\circ\rho)
-integrable from
\Delta
to
\mathbb{Z}
for all

\varepsilon>0
, where
\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x):={\rho\circ\log(x)}/{\left(\log\circ\rho\circ\log(x)\right)^{1+\varepsilon}}
.

We mention that the group
\Delta
given by the above theorem, is a diagonal product of lamplighter groups as defined by Brieussel-Zheng in
[
BZ21
]
.

Claim 13.2
.

The coupling given by the above theorem is optimal up to a log factor in the sense of
1.13
, namely: for every non-decreasing map
\varphi
if there exists a
(\varphi,L^{0})
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
\Delta
to
\mathbb{Z}
then
\varphi\preccurlyeq I_{\Delta}\simeq\rho\circ\log
.

Proof of the Claim.

Let
\Delta
be the group given by the above theorem. Assume that there exists a
(\varphi,L^{0})
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
\Delta
to
H
, for some non-decreasing map
\varphi
and let us prove that
\varphi\preccurlyeq I_{\Delta}\simeq\rho\circ\log
.

\bullet
Let us first prove that
x\mapsto x/\varphi(x)
is necessarily non-decreasing.

So assume towards a contradiction that
x\mapsto x/\varphi(x)
is decreasing. Then
\mathrm{id}\preccurlyeq\varphi
and thus any
\varphi
-integrable cocycle is at least
L^{1}
-integrable. Therefore the coupling is also
(L^{1},L^{0})
-integrable from
\Delta
to
H
. Thus, recalling that
I_{H}(x)\simeq x
and
I_{\Delta}\simeq\rho\circ\log
and using
12.2
, we get that in this case

x\simeq I_{H}(x)\preccurlyeq I_{\Delta}(x)\simeq\rho\circ\log(x)

But the map
x\mapsto x/\rho(x)
is non-decreasing, which contradicts the above inequality. Hence the map
x\mapsto x/\varphi(x)
has to be non-decreasing.

\bullet
Since
t\mapsto t/\varphi(t)
is non-decreasing, we can now apply
12.2
and obtain that
\varphi\circ I_{H}\preccurlyeq I_{\Delta}
.
Since
I_{H}\simeq\mathrm{id}
, the last inequality becomes
\varphi\preccurlyeq I_{\Delta}\simeq\rho\circ\log
. As wanted.
∎

The case of graph products

We now turn to the proof of the general case.
The existence of the group
G
and the pairing are given by
13.1
and
1.10
. The optimality is then obtained using
13.2
and
12.1
.

Proof of
1.13
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite simple graph that contains an untransvectable vertex, denoted by
v
, and
H
be right-angled Artin group over
\Gamma
.

Construction of the coupling

Let
\rho
and for all
\varepsilon>0
let
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
be as in the theorem. Then by
13.1
there exists a group
\Delta
admitting a
(\varphi_{\varepsilon},L^{0})
-integrable orbit equivalence pairing from
\Delta
to
\mathbb{Z}
.
Denote by
G
the graph product over
\Gamma
, with vertex groups
G_{v}=\Delta
and
G_{w}=H_{w}
for all
w\neq v
in
V\Gamma
. Then by
1.10
,
1.10
there exists a
(\varphi_{\varepsilon},L^{0})
-integrable orbit equivalence pairing from
G
to
H
.

Optimality

Let
\varphi
be a non-decreasing map and assume that we have a
(\varphi,L^{0})
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
G
to
H
. By the first point of
12.1
,
12.1
there exists
\sigma(v)\in V\Gamma
and a
(\varphi,L^{0})
-integrable measure equivalence coupling from
G_{v}=\Delta
to
H_{\sigma(v)}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
.
So applying
13.2
to the latter coupling leads
\varphi\preccurlyeq\rho\circ\log
. Therefore, the
(\varphi_{\varepsilon},L^{0})
-integrable coupling constructed in the first part of the proof is optimal up to a logarithmic error, as wanted.∎

Part IV
Rigidity under ergodicity assumptions

The goal of this part is to prove orbit equivalence rigidity theorems for actions of graph products that satisfy certain strong ergodicity assumptions. Our main theorems are Theorem
14.2
(giving a rigidity statement among actions of graph products) and Theorem
15.1
(giving a superrigidity statament, comparing two actions
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
where only
G
is a graph product, while
H
is an arbitrary countable group). This is in the vein of the work of Monod and Shalom for direct products
[
MS06
]
, and generalizes the case of right-angled Artin groups
[
HHI23
]
.

14
Rigidity among graph products

14.1
Some terminology

We start by recalling some terminology that will be used throughout the section.

Ergodicity assumptions.

Recall that a measure-preserving action of a countable group
G
on a standard probability space
(X,\mu)
is

—

weakly mixing
if the diagonal action of
G
on
X\times X
is ergodic.

—

totally ergodic
if every infinite subgroup of
G
acts ergodically on
X
.

—

mildly mixing
if it is ergodic and if for any measurable subset
U\subseteq X
with
\mu(U),\mu(X\setminus U)>0
, and any sequence of pairwise distinct elements
g_{n}
in
G
, one has
\liminf\mu(g_{n}U\triangle U)>0
. By
[
SW82
]
, this is equivalent to the following requirement, which is in fact the original definition of mild mixing due to Furstenberg and Weiss
[
FW78
]
: for every measure-preserving ergodic
G
-action on a standard Borel space
S
equipped with a non-atomic
\sigma
-finite Borel measure, the diagonal
G
-action on
X\times S
is ergodic.

—

strongly mixing
if given any two Borel subsets
U,V\subseteq X
, and any sequence
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in G^{\mathbb{N}}
of pairwise distinct elements, one has
\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mu(g_{n}U\cap V)=\mu(U)\mu(V)
.

We notice that strongly mixing actions are in particular mildly mixing, that mildly mixing actions of countably infinite groups are weakly mixing, and that weakly mixing actions are ergodic (and totally ergodic actions are also ergodic).

We also mention that if
G
has a subgroup
H
which acts weakly mixingly on
X
, then the
G
-action on
X
is weakly mixing. This is however not true for the total ergodicity, mild mixing and strong mixing conditions.

(Virtual) conjugation of actions.

Two measure-preserving actions
G\curvearrowright(X,\mu)
and
H\curvearrowright(Y,\nu)
on standard probability spaces are
conjugate
if there exist a group isomorphism
\theta:G\to H
and a measure space isomorphism
f:X\to Y
such that
f(g\cdot x)=\theta(g)f(x)
for every
g\in G
and almost every
x\in X
.

There exists a weaker notion of
virtually conjugate
actions, which takes into account the possibility of passing to finite-index subgroups and taking the quotient by a finite normal subgroup. We now recall the definition, see e.g.
[
Kid08b
, Definition 1.3]
.

First, given a countable group
G
, a finite-index subgroup
H\subseteq G
, and a measure-preserving action of
H
on a standard probability space
X
, there is a notion of an action of
G
on a standard finite measure space
\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}(X)
, induced from that of
H
, defined as follows. Let
\theta:H\to G
be the inclusion map. The space
X\times G
(where
G
is equipped with the counting measure) is equipped with a measure-preserving action of
H\times G
given by
(h,g)\cdot(x,g^{\prime})=(hx,\theta(h)g^{\prime}g^{-1})
. We then let
\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}(X):=H\backslash(X\times G)
: this is naturally a standard finite measure space (isomorphic to
X\times G/H
), which we renormalize to a probability space. The
G
-action on
X\times G
descends to a probability measure-preserving
G
-action on
\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}(X)
.

Notice for future use that, following the notation from
[
Vae07
, Notation 4.3]
, we can more generally define the induced action
G\curvearrowright\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}(X,\theta)
whenever
\theta:H\to G
is an injective homomorphism with finite-index image. In the particular case where
\theta
is an isomorphism, then the
G
-action on
X
and the
H
-action on
\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}(X,\theta)
are conjugate (via the isomorphism
\theta:H\to G
and the measure space isomorphism
x\mapsto[x,e]
, where
[x,e]
denotes the class of
(x,e)
).

Notice also that, if a countable group
G
has a measure-preserving action on a standard probability space
(X,\mu)
, and if
F_{G}\unlhd G
is a finite normal subgroup, then there is a quotient measure-preserving action of
G/F_{G}\curvearrowright(X,\mu)/F_{G}
, where
(X,\mu)/F_{G}
is the space of
F_{G}
-orbits in
X
, renormalized to a probability measure.

Two measure-preserving actions
G\curvearrowright(X,\mu)
and
H\curvearrowright(Y,\nu)
are
virtually conjugate
if we can find

—

short exact sequences

1\to F_{G}\to G\to G_{1}\to 1\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt1\to F_{H}\to H\to H_{1}\to 1,

where
F_{G}\unlhd G
and
F_{H}\unlhd H
are finite;

—

finite-index subgroups
G_{2}\subseteq G_{1}
and
H_{2}\subseteq H_{1}
;

—

and conjugate measure-preserving actions
G_{2}\curvearrowright(X_{2},\mu_{2})
and
H_{2}\curvearrowright(Y_{2},\nu_{2})
, such that

—

the action
G_{1}\curvearrowright(X,\mu)/F_{G}
is conjugate to
G_{1}\curvearrowright\mathrm{Ind}_{G_{2}}^{G_{1}}(X_{2})
, and

—

the action
H_{1}\curvearrowright(X,\mu)/F_{H}
is conjugate to
H_{1}\curvearrowright\mathrm{Ind}_{H_{2}}^{H_{1}}(X_{2})
.

14.2
Rigidity of actions within the class of graph products

We make the following definition.

Definition 14.1
.

Let
G
be a graph product of countable groups over a finite simple graph. A free, probability measure preserving action of
G
on a standard probability space
X
is
vertexwise ergodic
(resp.
vertexwise weakly mixing
, resp.
vertexwise totally ergodic
) if for every vertex group
G_{v}
, the action of
G_{v}
on
X
is ergodic (resp. weakly mixing, resp. totally ergodic).

When
G
is a graph product with countably infinite vertex groups, notice that vertexwise weak mixing and vertexwise total ergodicity are satisfied for all strongly mixing actions of
G
on
X
. Besides Bernoulli actions, this includes for example actions coming from embedding
G
as a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
with non-compact image, and using the Howe–Moore property – see
[
HW99
, Theorem 3.2]
for examples of faithful representations of graph products into
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
. See more generally
[
BdlN22
, Corollary B]
for constructions of faithful representations of graph products into
\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})
, assuming the linearity over
\mathbb{C}
of the vertex groups.

Recall that a group
G
has the
unique root property
if for every
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
and
n\geq 1
, if
g_{1}^{n}=g_{2}^{n}
, then
g_{1}=g_{2}
. In this section, we establish the following theorem. We refer to Section
3.1.1
for the notions of stable orbit equivalence and compression constants.

Theorem 14.2
.

Let
\Gamma_{G},\Gamma_{H}
be two connected finite simple graphs, with no vertex joined to every other vertex. Let
G,H
be graph products with countably infinite torsion-free vertex groups over
\Gamma_{G},\Gamma_{H}
, respectively. Let
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
be two free, vertexwise ergodic, measure-preserving actions on standard probability spaces. Assume that either

—

G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise weakly mixing, or

—

G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise totally ergodic, or

—

H
has the unique root property.

If there is a stable orbit equivalence
f
from
G\curvearrowright X
to
H\curvearrowright Y
with compression constant
\kappa(f)\geq 1
, then the two actions are conjugate (and every SOE cocycle
c:G\times X\to H
associated to
f
is cohomologous to a group isomorphism
G\to H
).

We mention that the last two cases will be useful in the next section to obtain superrigidity statements (
section

15
).

Remark 14.3

(Discussion on the connectedness hypothesis)
.

The assumption that the defining graph
\Gamma_{G}
is connected cannot be removed. In fact, Bernoulli actions of non-abelian free groups fail to satisfy the form of rigidity provided by Theorems
14.2
, in view of works of Bowen
[
Bow11a
,
Bow11b
]
.

Remark 14.4

(Examples of groups with the unique root property)
.

Assuming that
H
satisfies the unique root property is equivalent to assuming that each of its vertex groups satisfies the unique root property
[
BF19
, Theorem 6.5]
. The class of groups that satisfy this property is vast. It includes, among others, all torsion-free nilpotent groups
[
Mal49
]
, and more generally all bi-orderable groups (including all Baumslag–Solitar groups
\mathrm{BS}(1,n)
with
n\geq 1
, Thompson’s group or more generally diagram groups
[
GS06
]
), all torsion-free hyperbolic groups (using the fact that the centralizer of every non-trivial element is cyclic
[
BH99
, Corollary III.
\Gamma
.3.10]
) and more generally toral relatively hyperbolic groups (even more generally, torsion-free groups whose maximal abelian subgroups are malnormal, called
CSA
groups, which also include limit groups
[
MR96
, Proposition 8]
), and some torsion-free finite-index subgroups of surface mapping class groups
[
BP09
]
and
\mathrm{Out}(F_{N})

[
Gue22
]
. The unique root property is also stable under certain group-theoretic operations like taking wreath products
[
Bau60
]
.

Proof strategy and outline of the section.

Our proof of Theorem
14.2
follows the strategy used by Huang, Ioana and the second-named author in
[
HHI23
]
. In particular we will follow very closely the presentation of
[
HHI23
, Propositions 6.1 and 6.2]
. The idea is the following. Let
c
be a stable orbit equivalence cocycle associated to
f
.

—

We first untwist
c
(i.e. change it within its cohomology class) to a group homomorphism on some vertex group of
G
. The main tools are our recognition statements from Section
7
, ensuring in particular that the orbit equivalence relation “remembers” the maximal product parabolic subgroups, together with the work of Monod–Shalom on direct products
[
MS06
]
.

—

Once this is done, a propagation argument enables to untwist the cocycle on the whole group
G
. We give three versions of this propagation argument: one uses weak mixing at the level of the vertex groups, one uses total ergodicity, and the third relies on the unique root property for
H
.

The section is organized as follows. After presenting the propagation arguments in Section
14.2.1
, we review and establish a few extra tools in Sections
14.2.2
to
14.2.4
, and complete our proof of Theorem
14.2
in Section
14.2.5
.

14.2.1
Three propagation arguments

Propagation via weak mixing.

The idea of exploiting weak mixing to propagate rigidity from a normal subgroup to an ambient group arose in the work of Popa, see
[
Pop07
, Lemma 3.6]
, which finds its roots in
[
Pop06b
, Lemma 5.7]
. Popa’s arguments were formulated in the language of von Neumann algebras; we will use Furman’s formulation
[
Fur07
, Lemma 3.5]
whose statement is more ergodic-theoretic.

Lemma 14.5
.

Let
G,H
be countable groups. Let
G\curvearrowright X
be a measure-preserving action on a standard probability space
X
, and
c:G\times X\to H
be a cocycle. Let
G_{1},\dots,G_{k}
be subgroups of
G
satisfying the following three assumptions:

—

the set
G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{k}
generates
G
;

—

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, the subgroup
G_{j+1}
normalizes
G_{j}
;

—

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, the subgroup
G_{j}
acts weakly mixingly on
X
.

Assume that there exists a group homomorphism
\theta_{1}:G_{1}\to H
such that for every
g\in G_{1}
and almost every
x\in X
, one has
c(g,x)=\theta_{1}(g)
.

Then there exists a group homomorphism
\theta:G\to H
such that for every
g\in G
and almost every
x\in X
, one has
c(g,x)=\theta(g)
.

Proof.

We first prove by induction that for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, there exists a group homomorphism
\theta_{j}:G_{j}\to H
such that for every
g\in G_{j}
and almost every
x\in X
, one has
c(g,x)=\theta_{j}(g)
. This holds when
j=1
by assumption. And assuming this holds for
G_{j}
, then
[
Fur07
, Lemma 3.5]
, applied to the subgroup
G_{j}
of
G
, with
Y
reduced to a point and
\rho=\theta_{j}
, ensures that it also holds for
G_{j+1}
.

Now, as
G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{k}
generates
G
, using the cocycle relation, we deduce that for every
g\in G
, the map
c(g,\cdot)
is almost everywhere constant. Since
G
is countable, there exists a conull Borel subset
X^{*}\subseteq X
such that for every
g\in G
, the map
c(g,\cdot)_{|X^{*}}
is constant. And the cocycle relation ensures that the map
\theta
sending
g
to the constant value of
c(g,\cdot)_{|X^{*}}
is a group homomorphism, as desired.
∎

Propagation via ergodicity and unique roots.

The following lemma exploits the unique root property to propagate rigidity. It is a very slight variation over
[
HHI23
, Lemma 4.2]
.

Lemma 14.6
.

Let
G,H
be countable groups, and assume that
H
satisfies the unique root property. Let
G\curvearrowright X
be a measure-preserving action on a standard probability space
X
, and
c:G\times X\to H
be a cocycle. Let
G_{1},\dots,G_{k}
be subgroups of
G
satisfying the following three assumptions:

—

the set
G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{k}
generates
G
;

—

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, the subgroup
G_{j+1}
centralizes
G_{j}
;

—

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, the subgroup
G_{j}
acts ergodically on
X
.

Assume that there exists a group homomorphism
\theta_{1}:G_{1}\to H
such that for every
g\in G_{1}
and almost every
x\in X
, one has
c(g,x)=\theta_{1}(g)
.

Then there exists a group homomorphism
\theta:G\to H
such that for every
g\in G
and almost every
x\in X
, one has
c(g,x)=\theta(g)
.

Proof.

As in the previous proof, it is enough to prove by induction that for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, there exists a group homomorphism
\theta_{j}:G_{j}\to H
such that for every
g\in G_{j}
and almost every
x\in X
, one has
c(g,x)=\theta_{j}(g)
. In fact, it is enough to prove that for every
g\in G_{j}
, the map
c(g,\cdot)
is essentially constant: indeed, the map sending
g
to the essential value of
c(g,\cdot)
is then automatically a group homomorphism, in view of the cocycle relation.

This holds for
j=1
by assumption. Assume that it holds for some
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, and let
g\in G_{j+1}
. Let
X=\sqcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
be a countable Borel partition such that for every
i\in I
, the map
c(g,\cdot)_{|X_{i}}
is constant, with value denoted by
h_{i}\in H
(see also
fig.

22
). We aim to prove that
h_{i_{1}}=h_{i_{2}}
for any
i_{1},i_{2}\in I
.

Figure 22
:
On
X_{i}
the value of
c(g,\cdot)
is constant, equal to
h_{i}
.

So let
i_{1},i_{2}\in I
. Since
G_{j}
acts ergodically on
X
, there exists
g^{\prime}\in G_{j}
such that
g^{\prime}X_{i_{1}}\cap X_{i_{2}}=:V
has positive measure. Let also
U:=(g^{\prime})^{-1}(V)
(see also
fig.

23
). Since
g\in G_{j+1}
and
g^{\prime}\in G_{j}
, we have
gg^{\prime}=g^{\prime}g
. So for every
x\in U
, we have
gg^{\prime}\cdot x=g^{\prime}g\cdot x
. Applying the cocycle
c
gives

c(g,g^{\prime}\cdot x)c(g^{\prime},x)=c(g^{\prime},g\cdot x)c(g,x),

in other words

h_{i_{2}}\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})=\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})h_{i_{1}}.

(14.1)

On the other hand, using Poincaré recurrence, there also exists
k>0
such that the subset
X_{i_{1}}\cap(g^{\prime})^{k}X_{i_{1}}=:Z
has positive measure. Let
W:=(g^{\prime})^{-k}(Z)
(see also
fig.

24
). Then for every
x\in W
, we have
g(g^{\prime})^{k}\cdot x=(g^{\prime})^{k}g\cdot x
, so applying the cocycle
c
gives

c\big(g,(g^{\prime})^{k}\cdot x\big)c\big((g^{\prime})^{k},x\big)=c\big((g^{\prime})^{k},g\cdot x\big)c(g,x),

in other words

h_{i_{1}}\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})^{k}=\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})^{k}h_{i_{1}}.

(14.2)

This rewrites as
\left(h_{i_{1}}\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})h_{i_{1}}^{-1}\right)^{k}=\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})^{k}
. Using the unique root property for
H
, we deduce that
h_{i_{1}}
and
\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})
commute. Therefore, Equation (
14.1
) yields
h_{i_{2}}=h_{i_{1}}
, as desired.
∎

We represent here how we obtain
eq.

14.1
. In
orange
are represented the actions of elements in
G
, while in
blue
are written the corresponding values of the cocycle
c
. The sets
U
and
V
are drawn in
brown
.

Figure 23
:
Illustration for
eq.

14.1

Figure 24
:
Illustration for
eq.

14.2

Propagation via total ergodicity.

The following lemma uses total ergodicity to propagate rigidity. The argument is a slight variation over the previous one, but does not necessit to assume that
H
satisfies the unique root property.

Lemma 14.7
.

Let
G,H
be countable groups, and assume that
G
is torsion-free. Let
G\curvearrowright X
be a measure-preserving action on a standard probability space
X
, and let
c:G\times X\to H
be a cocycle. Let
G_{1},\dots,G_{k}
be subgroups of
G
satisfying the following three assumptions:

—

the set
G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{k}
generates
G
;

—

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, the subgroup
G_{j+1}
centralizes
G_{j}
;

—

for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, the subgroup
G_{j}
acts totally ergodically on
X
.

Assume that there exists a group homomorphism
\theta_{1}:G_{1}\to H
such that for every
g\in G_{1}
and almost every
x\in X
, one has
c(g,x)=\theta_{1}(g)
.

Then there exists a group homomorphism
\theta:G\to H
such that for every
g\in G
and almost every
x\in X
, one has
c(g,x)=\theta(g)
.

Proof.

As in the previous proof, it is enough to prove by induction that for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and every
g\in G_{j}
the map
c(g,\cdot)
is essentially constant.

This holds for
j=1
by assumption. Assume that it holds for some
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, and let
g\in G_{j+1}
. Let
X=\sqcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
be a countable Borel partition such that for every
i\in I
, the map
c(g,\cdot)_{|X_{i}}
