is constant, with value denoted by
h_{i}\in H
(as in
fig.

22
,
fig.

22
). We aim to prove that
h_{i_{1}}=h_{i_{2}}
for any
i_{1},i_{2}\in I
.

For any
i_{1},i_{2}\in I
, as in the previous proof (see discussion above Equation (
14.1
)), the ergodicity of the action of
G_{j}
enables us to find
g^{\prime}\in G_{j}
such that

h_{i_{2}}\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})=\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})h_{i_{1}}.

(14.3)

And as in the previous proof (see the discussion above Equation (
14.2
)), we can also find
k>0
such that

h_{i_{1}}\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})^{k}=\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})^{k}h_{i_{1}}.

(14.4)

Since
G
is torsion-free and
G_{j}
acts totally ergodically on
X
, in particular
(g^{\prime})^{k}
acts ergodically on
X
. So let
m>0
be such that
(g^{\prime})^{km}X_{i_{1}}\cap(g^{\prime})^{-1}(X_{i_{1}})
has positive measure. In particular, there exists a positive measure Borel subset
U\subseteq X_{i_{1}}
such that
(g^{\prime})^{km+1}(U)\subseteq X_{i_{1}}
. For almost every
x\in U
, since
gg^{\prime}=g^{\prime}g
, we have

c\left(g,(g^{\prime})^{km+1}\cdot x\right)c\left((g^{\prime})^{km+1},x\right)=c\left((g^{\prime})^{km+1},g\cdot x\right)c(g,x),

which rewrites as

h_{i_{1}}\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})^{km+1}=\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})^{km+1}h_{i_{1}}.

(14.5)

Equation (
14.4
) implies that
(h_{i_{1}}\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})h_{i_{1}}^{-1})^{km}=\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})^{km}
, and Equation (
14.5
) gives

(h_{i_{1}}\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})h_{i_{1}}^{-1})^{km+1}=\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})^{km+1}.

Combining these two equations yields
h_{i_{1}}\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})h_{i_{1}}^{-1}=\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})
. In other words
h_{i_{1}}
and
\theta_{j}(g^{\prime})
commute. Therefore, Equation (
14.3
) yields
h_{i_{2}}=h_{i_{1}}
, as desired.
∎

Untwisting the cocycle on one vertex group is enough.

The following lemma, which is essentially
[
HHI23
, Lemma 4.3]
, reduces the proof of Theorem
14.2
to untwisting the SOE cocycle to a group homomorphism on one vertex group.

Lemma 14.8
.

Let
G,H
be two graph products with countably infinite vertex groups over connected finite simple graphs
\Gamma_{G},\Gamma_{H}
with trivial clique factors. Let
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
be two free, ergodic, measure-preserving actions on standard probability spaces.
Assume that either

—

G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise weakly mixing or

—

G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise totally ergodic, and
G
is torsion-free, or

—

G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise ergodic, and
H
has the unique root property.

Assume that there exists a stable orbit equivalence
f
from
G\curvearrowright X
to
H\curvearrowright Y
, with
\kappa(f)\geq 1
, and let
c:G\times X\to H
be an SOE cocycle associated to
f
.

Assume that
c
is cohomologous to a cocycle
c^{\prime}
for which there exist
v\in V\Gamma_{G}
and a group homomorphism
\theta_{v}:G_{v}\to H
, such that for every
g\in G_{v}
and almost every
x\in X
, one has
c^{\prime}(g,x)=\theta_{v}(g)
.

Then the actions
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
are conjugate (and
c
is cohomologous to an isomorphism).

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to that of
[
HHI23
, Lemma 4.3]
, we include it for the convenience of the reader.

Since
\Gamma_{G}
is connected, we can write
\{G_{w}\}_{w\in V\Gamma_{G}}=\{G_{1},\dots,G_{k}\}
(possibly with repetitions among the
G_{j}
) in such a way that for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, the group
G_{j+1}
centralizes
G_{j}
. We can further assume that
G_{1}=G_{v}
, with
v\in V\Gamma_{G}
as in the statement of the lemma. It follows that the subgroups
G_{1},\dots,G_{k}
satisfy all the assumptions from either Lemma
14.5
(if we had assumed that
G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise weakly mixing) or Lemma
14.6
(if we had assumed that
G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise ergodic and
H
has the unique root property), or Lemma
14.7
(if we had assumed that
G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise totally ergodic and
G
is torsion-free).

Lemma
14.5
(or Lemma
14.6
, or Lemma
14.7
) therefore implies that
c
is cohomologous to a group homomorphism
\theta:G\to H
. As the clique factor of
\Gamma_{G}
is trivial,
G
has no non-trivial finite normal subgroup (see e.g.
[
MV22
, Corollary 2.9]
). It thus follows from
[
Vae07
, Lemma 4.7 and its proof]
that
\theta
is injective,
\theta(G)
has finite index in
H
, the action
H\curvearrowright Y
is conjugate to the
H
-action on
\mathrm{Ind}_{G}^{H}(X,\theta)
, and
\kappa(f)=\frac{1}{[H:\theta(G)]}
. As
\kappa(f)\geq 1
, we deduce that
[H:\theta(G)]=1
. In particular
\theta
is a group isomorphism, so the actions
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
are conjugate, as desired.
∎

14.2.2
Combinatorial tools

The following two lemmas are restatements of
[
HHI23
, Lemmas 1.4 and 1.5]
. In
[
HHI23
]
, these two results are formulated for right-angled Artin groups, however their proofs do not use the structure of the vertex groups, and are also valid for graph products. The second case in the following lemma is illustrated in Figure
25
.

Lemma 14.9

(
[
HHI23
, Lemmas 1.4 and 1.5]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph product over a connected finite simple graph
\Gamma
, such that
\Gamma
contains no vertex joined to every other vertex. Then

—

either
G
contains a maximal product parabolic subgroup with trivial clique factor;

—

or else
G
contains two maximal product parabolic subgroups
P_{1},P_{2}
with non-trivial clique factors
C_{1},C_{2}
, with
C_{2}\subseteq C_{1}^{\perp}
and
C_{1}\subseteq C_{2}^{\perp}
.

Lemma 14.10

(
[
HHI23
, Lemma 1.5]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph product over a finite simple graph, and let
P_{1},P_{2}\subseteq G
be two distinct maximal product parabolic subgroups, with respective clique factors
C_{1},C_{2}
.
Then
C_{1}\cap C_{2}=\{1\}
.

Figure 25
:
Example of products with commuting clique factors

14.2.3
Groupoid tools: Detecting products with trivial clique factor

We refer to
6.1
for the notion of a strongly reduced graph. The following lemma is a variation over the results presented in Section
7
of the present work – the reader is refered to this section for all relevant definitions, in particular Definition
7.8
,
7.8
for Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{cofact}})
. It gives a groupoid theoretic characterization of product parabolic subgroups with non-trivial clique factor.

Lemma 14.11
.

Let
G
be a graph product of countably infinite groups over a strongly reduced finite simple graph
\Gamma
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space
X
, equipped with an action-like cocycle
\rho:\mathcal{G}\to G
. Let
P
be a product parabolic subgroup whose type is not a clique. Let
\mathcal{P}=\rho^{-1}(P)
.

Then
P
has a non-trivial clique factor if and only if given any non-empty finite set
\{\mathcal{Q}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{Q}_{n}\}
of measured subgroupoids of
\mathcal{P}
, if
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}_{j})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{cofact}})
for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, then
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\mathcal{Q}_{n}
is of infinite type.

Proof.

Write
P=C_{0}\times F_{1}\times\dots\times F_{k}
, where
C_{0}
is the clique factor of
P
, and the
F_{j}
do not further split as direct products of parabolic subgroups. We have
k\geq 1
because the type of
P
is not a clique.

We first assume that
C_{0}\neq\{1\}
. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{Q}_{n}
be finitely many measured subgroupoids of
\mathcal{G}
such that the triples
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}_{j})
all satisfy Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{cofact}})
. Since the type of
P
is not a clique and
\rho
is action-like, the pair
(\mathcal{P},\rho)
is nowhere of clique type (Lemma
4.14
,
4.14
). Therefore, we can apply Lemma
7.9
,
7.9
and deduce that for every
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
(\mathcal{Q}_{j},\rho)
is of clique-inclusive co-factor type within
(\mathcal{P},\rho)
. Thus, up to a conull Borel subset of
X
and a countable Borel partition, we have
\rho^{-1}(C_{0})\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\mathcal{Q}_{n}
. In particular
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\mathcal{Q}_{n}
is of infinite type.

We now assume that
C_{0}=\{1\}
. For every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, let

\mathcal{Q}_{j}=\rho^{-1}(F_{1}\times\dots\times\hat{F}_{j}\times\dots\times F_{k}).

By Lemma
7.9
,
7.9
for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, the triple
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}_{j})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{cofact}})
. In addition
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cap\dots\cap\mathcal{Q}_{n}
is trivial.
∎

Lemma 14.12
.

Let
G,H
be two graph products with countably infinite vertex groups, over finite simple graphs
\Gamma_{G},\Gamma_{H}
which are not cliques. If
G
and
H
are measure equivalent and
\Gamma_{G}
is reducible, then
\Gamma_{H}
is reducible.

Proof.

Since
G
and
H
are measure equivalent, we can find a measured groupoid
\mathcal{G}
over a standard probability space
X
, equipped with two action-like cocycles
\rho_{G}:\mathcal{G}\to G
and
\rho_{H}:\mathcal{G}\to H
(see Example
4.3
,
4.3
, and Lemma
4.12
,
4.12
). Since
\Gamma_{G}
is reducible,
(\mathcal{G},\rho_{G})
itself is of maximal product type within
(\mathcal{G},\rho_{G})
. And it is not of isolated clique type because
\Gamma_{G}
is not a clique. By Lemma
7.4
(1),
7.4
applied to the cocycle
\rho_{G}
, the pair
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G})
satisfies Property
(\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{prod}})
. And by Lemma
7.4
(2) applied to the cocycle
\rho_{H}
, we deduce that
(\mathcal{G},\rho_{H})
is also of maximal product type (it is nowhere contained in a subgroupoid of isolated clique type because
\Gamma_{H}
is not a clique). This implies that
\Gamma_{H}
is reducible, as desired.
∎

14.2.4
A theorem of Monod–Shalom

We will need the following theorem which comes from the work of Monod–Shalom (see
[
MS06
, p. 862]
). Its formulation comes from
[
HHI23
]
.

Theorem 14.13

(Monod–Shalom, see
[
HHI23
, Lemma 3.1]
)
.

Let
G,H
be countable groups. Let
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
be free ergodic measure-preserving actions on standard probability spaces. Let
f:U\to V
be a stable orbit equivalence from
G\curvearrowright X
to
H\curvearrowright Y
(where
U\subseteq X
and
V\subseteq Y
are positive measure Borel subsets). Let
c:G\times X\to H
be an SOE cocycle associated to
f
.

Let
A\unlhd G
and
B\unlhd H
be normal subgroups acting ergodically on
X,Y
, and assume that for every
x\in U
, one has
f((A\cdot x)\cap U)=(B\cdot f(x))\cap V
.

Then there exist a group isomorphism
\alpha:G/A\to H/B
, and a cocycle
c^{\prime}:G\times X\to H
such that

—

c^{\prime}
is
H
-cohomologous to
c
;

—

for every
g\in G
and almost every
x\in X
, if
x,g\cdot x\in U
, then
c^{\prime}(g,x)=c(g,x)
;

—

for every
g\in G
and almost every
x\in X
, one has
c^{\prime}(g,x)\in\alpha(gA)
.

14.2.5
End of the proof

We now complete our proof of Theorem
14.2
. We follow the proof of
[
HHI23
, Propositions 6.1 and 6.2]
very closely, with only a few departures in the presentation (in particular, we have decided to treat the cases corresponding to
[
HHI23
, Proposition 6.1]
and
[
HHI23
, Proposition 6.2]
in a harmonized way).

Proof of Theorem
14.2
.

We will prove the following: if
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
are vertexwise ergodic free, probability measure-preserving actions, and if
c:G\times X\to H
is an SOE cocycle, then there exists a vertex group
G_{v}\subseteq G
such that
c_{|G_{v}\times X}
is
H
-cohomologous to a group homomorphism
G_{v}\to H
. In view of Lemma
14.8
, this will be enough to conclude our proof.

By collapsing some subgraphs if necessary, we can write
G
and
H
as graph products over finite simple graphs
\bar{\Gamma}_{G}
and
\bar{\Gamma}_{H}
that satisfy the following two conditions:

—

\bar{\Gamma}_{G}
and
\bar{\Gamma}_{H}
are joins (possibly with only one join factor) of strongly reduced graphs in the sense of Definition
6.1
,
6.1
.

—

The graph
\bar{\Gamma}_{G}
does not contain any vertex
v
such that
\bar{\Gamma}_{G}=\mathrm{star}(v)
, and neither does
\bar{\Gamma}_{H}
.

The vertex groups for the graph product structure of
G
over
\bar{\Gamma}_{G}
contain those for its graph product structure over
\Gamma_{G}
, so in particular the action of
G\curvearrowright X
is still vertexwise ergodic with respect to the graph product structure over
\bar{\Gamma}_{G}
(and likewise for
H
). Therefore
\bar{\Gamma}_{G}
and
\bar{\Gamma}_{H}
still satisfy all assumptions from Theorem
14.2
. Thus, from now on, we will assume without loss of generality that
\Gamma_{G}
and
\Gamma_{H}
are joins of strongly reduced graphs to start with.

Let
U\subseteq X
and
V\subseteq Y
be the positive measure Borel subsets defined as the source and range of the stable orbit equivalence
f
. In other words
f
is a measure-scaling isomorphism
U\to V
that sends orbits (restricted to
U
) to orbits (restricted to
V
). After renormalizing the measures on
U
and
V
to probability measures, the groupoid
(G\ltimes X)_{|U}
is isomorphic (via
f
) to
(H\ltimes Y)_{|V}
. We denote by
\mathcal{G}
this common groupoid, which naturally comes equipped with two cocycles
\rho_{G}:\mathcal{G}\to G
and
\rho_{H}:\mathcal{G}\to H
, which are action-like in view of Lemma
4.12
,
4.12
and Remark
4.11
,
4.11
. We view
\mathcal{G}
as a measured groupoid over
U
.

Our proof has three steps.

Step 1.
We show that there exist

—

parabolic subgroups
P_{G}^{1},P_{G}^{2}\subseteq G
, and
P_{H}^{1},P_{H}^{2}\subseteq H
,

—

normal infinite parabolic subgroups
C_{G}^{i}\unlhd P_{G}^{i}
and
C_{H}^{i}\unlhd P_{H}^{i}
for every
i\in\{1,2\}
, such that

—

C_{G}^{1}
and
C_{G}^{2}
intersect trivially and commute,

—

C_{H}^{1}
and
C_{H}^{2}
intersect trivially and commute,

—

for every
i\in\{1,2\}
, one has
P_{G}^{i}=C_{G}^{i}\times(C_{G}^{i})^{\perp}
and
P_{H}^{i}=C_{H}^{i}\times(C_{H}^{i})^{\perp}
,

—

a positive measure Borel subset
W\subseteq U
, such that

—

for every
i\in\{1,2\}
, one has
\rho_{G}^{-1}(P_{G}^{i})_{|W}=\rho_{H}^{-1}(P_{H}^{i})_{|W}
,

—

for every
i\in\{1,2\}
, one has
\rho_{G}^{-1}(C_{G}^{i})_{|W}=\rho_{H}^{-1}(C_{H}^{i})_{|W}
.

The proof of Step 1 decomposes into three cases.

Case 1:
The graph
\Gamma_{G}
decomposes non-trivially as a join.

Write
\Gamma_{G}=\Gamma_{1}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{k}
, where each
\Gamma_{j}
does not split further as a join – notice that no
\Gamma_{j}
is reduced to one vertex, as
\Gamma_{G}
is not contained in the star of a vertex by assumption. Lemma
14.12
ensures that
\Gamma_{H}
also splits as a join: we write
\Gamma_{H}=\Lambda_{1}\circ\dots\circ\Lambda_{n}
in such a way that no
\Lambda_{j}
further splits as a join. Here again our assumption on
\Gamma_{H}
ensures that no
\Lambda_{j}
is reduced to one vertex.

We let
P_{G}^{1}=P_{G}^{2}=G
, and
P_{H}^{1}=P_{H}^{2}=H
, and we let
C_{G}^{1}
and
C_{G}^{2}
be two different factors of
G
(so
C_{G}^{1}
and
C_{G}^{2}
intersect trivially and commute).

We now apply Lemma
8.5
,
lemma

8.5
. This lemma gives a characterization of being of factor type with no reference to any action-like cocycle, and therefore it ensures that there exists a positive measure Borel subset
W\subseteq U
, and distinct factors
C_{H}^{1},C_{H}^{2}
of
H
, such that
\rho_{G}^{-1}(C_{G}^{i})_{|W}=\rho_{H}^{-1}(C_{H}^{i})_{|W}
for every
i\in\{1,2\}
. This completes Step 1 in this case.

Case 2:
The graph
\Gamma_{G}
does not decompose non-trivially as a join, and
G
contains a maximal product parabolic subgroup
P_{G}
with trivial clique factor.

We let
P_{G}^{1}=P_{G}^{2}:=P_{G}
, and let
C_{G}^{1}
and
C_{G}^{2}
be two distinct factors of
P_{G}
(so
C_{G}^{1}
and
C_{G}^{2}
commute and intersect trivially). Notice that the maximality of
P_{G}
ensures that
P_{G}=C_{G}^{i}\times(C_{G}^{i})^{\perp}
for every
i\in\{1,2\}
.

By Lemma
14.12
, the graph
\Gamma_{H}
does not decompose non-trivially as a join either. And since
\Gamma_{G}
and
\Gamma_{H}
do not contain isolated cliques (because they are connected), there is no subgroupoid of
\mathcal{G}
of isolated clique type for either
\rho_{G}
or
\rho_{H}
, even in restriction to a positive measure subset. Lemma
7.4
,
7.4
therefore implies that we can find a positive measure Borel subset
V\subseteq U
, and a maximal product parabolic subgroup
P_{H}\subseteq H
, such that
\rho^{-1}(P_{G})_{|W}=\rho_{H}^{-1}(P_{H})_{|W}
. We let
P_{H}^{1}=P_{H}^{2}:=P_{H}
.

We now apply Lemma
7.11
,
lemma

7.11
. This lemma gives a characterization of being of factor type with no reference to any action-like cocycle, and therefore it ensures that, up to replacing
W
by a positive measure Borel subset, there exist two distinct factors
C_{H}^{1},C_{H}^{2}
of
P_{H}
such that
\rho^{-1}(C_{H}^{i})_{|W}=\rho^{-1}(C_{G}^{i})_{|W}
for every
i\in\{1,2\}
. And the maximality of
P_{H}
ensures that
P_{H}=C_{H}^{i}\times(C_{H}^{i})^{\perp}
for every
i\in\{1,2\}
. This completes the proof of Step 1 in this case.

Case 3:
The graph
\Gamma_{G}
does not decompose non-trivially as a join, and
G
does not contain any maximal product parabolic subgroup
P_{G}
with trivial clique factor.

In this case, Lemma
14.9
ensures that
G
contains two distinct maximal product parabolic subgroups
P_{G}^{1},P_{G}^{2}
whose clique factors
C_{G}^{1},C_{G}^{2}
are non-trivial and commute; in this case
C_{G}^{1}\cap C_{G}^{2}=\{1\}
by Lemma
14.10
. For every
i\in\{1,2\}
, the maximality of
P_{G}^{i}
ensures that
P_{G}^{i}=C_{G}^{i}\times(C_{G}^{i})^{\perp}
.

Again by Lemma
14.12
, the graph
\Gamma_{H}
does not decompose non-trivially as a join either.

Since
\Gamma_{G}
has no isolated clique, Lemma
7.4
,
7.4
gives a characterization of being of maximal product type that does not depend on any action-like cocycle. Since
\Gamma_{H}
has no isolated clique, Lemma
7.4
, applied to the cocycle
\rho_{H}
, therefore ensures that there exist maximal product parabolic subgroups
P_{H}^{1},P_{H}^{2}
and a positive measure Borel subset
W\subseteq U
such that for every
i\in\{1,2\}
, we have
\rho_{G}^{-1}(P_{G}^{i})_{|W}=\rho_{H}^{-1}(P_{H}^{i})_{|W}
. Notice that, since
P_{G}^{1}\neq P_{G}^{2}
, we have
\rho_{G}^{-1}(P_{G}^{1})_{|W}\neq\rho_{G}^{-1}(P_{G}^{2})_{|W}
(see Lemma
4.14
,
4.14
), and therefore
P_{H}^{1}\neq P_{H}^{2}
.

By Lemma
14.11
, the fact that
P_{G}^{i}
has a non-trivial clique factor is characterized by a groupoid-theoretic property of
\rho_{G}^{-1}(P_{G}^{i})
which is independent from the action-like cocycle. It follows that
P_{H}^{1}
and
P_{H}^{2}
also have non-trivial clique factors, which we denote respectively by
C_{H}^{1}
and
C_{H}^{2}
. By Lemma
14.10
, we have
C_{H}^{1}\cap C_{H}^{2}=\{1\}
.

Likewise, Lemma
7.11
, which characterizes the clique factor by a groupoid-theoretic property, ensures that, up to replacing
W
by a positive measure Borel subset, we have
\rho_{G}^{-1}(C_{G}^{i})_{|W}=\rho_{H}^{-1}(C_{H}^{i})_{|W}
for every
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Since
C_{G}^{1}
and
C_{G}^{2}
commute, we deduce that
\rho_{G}^{-1}(C_{G}^{1})_{|W}
and
\rho_{G}^{-1}(C_{G}^{2})_{|W}
normalize each other, and thus the groups
C_{H}^{1}
and
C_{H}^{2}
commute (as in the proof of Lemma
5.4
,
5.4
).

Finally, the maximality of
P_{H}^{1}
and
P_{H}^{2}
as product parabolic subgroups ensures that
P_{H}^{1}=C_{H}^{1}\times(C_{H}^{1})^{\perp}
and
P_{H}^{2}=C_{H}^{2}\times(C_{H}^{2})^{\perp}
.

Step 2.
We show that for every
i\in\{1,2\}
, there exist

—

a group homomorphism
\alpha_{i}:P_{G}^{i}\to P_{H}^{i}
, which restricts to an isomorphism from
C_{G}^{i}
to
C_{H}^{i}
, with
(\alpha_{1})_{|C_{G}^{1}\times C_{G}^{2}}=(\alpha_{2})_{|C_{G}^{1}\times C_{G}^{2}}
, and

—

measurable maps
\varphi_{i}:X\to H
and
\kappa_{i}:P_{G}^{i}\times X\to C_{H}^{i}
,

such that

c(g,x)=\varphi_{i}(g\cdot x)\alpha_{i}(g)\kappa_{i}(g,x)\varphi_{i}(x)^{-1},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall g\in P_{G}^{i},\penalty\ \text{a.e.\penalty\ }x\in X.

(14.6)

Informally, this means that we have untwisted the cocycle on
P_{G}^{i}
to a group homomorphism, “up to an error in
C_{H}^{i}
”, for every
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Up to replacing
c
by a cohomologous cocycle, we can assume that
c:G\times X\to H
is an SOE cocycle associated to
f_{|W}
, and that whenever
x,g\cdot x\in W
, the element
c(g,x)
is the unique element
h\in H
such that
f(g\cdot x)=h\cdot f(x)
. Recall also that
P_{G}^{i}
acts ergodically on
X
, and that
P_{G}^{i}
and
P_{H}^{i}
have the same orbits in restriction to
W
(because
\rho_{G}^{-1}(P_{G}^{i})_{|W}=\rho_{H}^{-1}(P_{H}^{i})_{|W}
). It follows that
c
is cohomologous to a cocycle
c_{i}:G\times X\to H
having the following two properties (see e.g. the discussion at the end of Section
3.1.2
,
section

3.1.2
):

—

(c_{i})_{|P_{G}^{i}\times X}
is an SOE cocycle associated to
f_{|W}
, viewed as a stable orbit equivalence between the actions
P_{G}^{i}\curvearrowright X
and
P_{H}^{i}\curvearrowright Y
,

—

c(g,x)=c_{i}(g,x)
whenever
x,g\cdot x\in W
.

For every
i\in\{1,2\}
, the normal subgroups
C_{G}^{i}\unlhd P_{G}^{i}
and
C_{H}^{i}\unlhd P_{H}^{i}
act ergodically on
X
and on
Y
. In addition
C_{G}^{i}
and
C_{H}^{i}
have the same orbits in restriction to
W
(because
\rho_{G}^{-1}(C_{G}^{i})_{|W}=\rho_{H}^{-1}(C_{H}^{i})_{|W}
). We can therefore apply Theorem
14.13
to the stable orbit equivalence
f_{|W}
, and deduce that, up to replacing
c_{i}
by a cohomologous cocycle, we can assume that the following hold:

—

there is a group isomorphism
\bar{\alpha}_{i}:P_{G}^{i}/C_{G}^{i}\to P_{H}^{i}/C_{H}^{i}
such that for every
g\in P_{G}^{i}
and almost every
x\in X
, one has
c_{i}(g,x)\in\bar{\alpha}_{i}\left(gC_{G}^{i}\right)
;

—

one has
c_{i}(g,x)=c(g,x)
whenever
x,g\cdot x\in W
.

Let
r_{i}:P_{H}^{i}\to(C_{H}^{i})^{\perp}
be the retraction (sending
C_{H}^{i}
to
\{1\}
). Let

c^{\prime}_{i}=(r_{i}\circ c_{i})_{|(C_{G}^{i})^{\perp}\times X},

a cocycle with values in
(C_{H}^{i})^{\perp}
. Through the natural isomorphisms
P_{G}^{i}/C_{G}^{i}\to(C_{G}^{i})^{\perp}
and
P_{H}^{i}/C_{H}^{i}\to(C_{H}^{i})^{\perp}
(given by the choice of the unique representative),
\bar{\alpha}_{i}
yields an isomorphism
\alpha_{i}:(C_{G}^{i})^{\perp}\to(C_{H}^{i})^{\perp}
such that

c^{\prime}_{i}(g,x)=\alpha_{i}(g),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall g\in(C_{G}^{i})^{\perp},\penalty\ \text{a.e\penalty\ }x\in X.

(14.7)

Recall that
C_{G}^{1}\subseteq(C_{G}^{2})^{\perp}
(because the parabolic subgroups
C_{G}^{1}
and
C_{G}^{2}
intersect trivially and commute), and
C_{H}^{1}\subseteq(C_{H}^{2})^{\perp}
. We will now prove the following claim.

Claim 14.14
.

We have
\alpha_{2}(C_{G}^{1})=C_{H}^{1}
.

Proof of the Claim.

We first prove that
\alpha_{2}(C_{G}^{1})\subseteq C_{H}^{1}
. So let
g\in C_{G}^{1}
. By Poincaré recurrence, for almost every
x\in W
, there exists
n>0
such that
g^{n}\cdot x\in W
. Since
x,g^{n}\cdot x\in W
, our definition of the cocycles
c_{i}
ensures that
c_{2}(g^{n},x)=c(g^{n},x)
. Since
C_{G}^{1}
and
C_{H}^{1}
have the same orbits in restriction to
W
, we deduce that
c_{2}(g^{n},x)\in C_{H}^{1}
, so
c_{2}(g^{n},x)=c^{\prime}_{2}(g^{n},x)
. Since
g^{n}
belongs to
C_{G}^{1}
, which is contained in
(C_{G}^{2})^{\perp}
, our definition of
\alpha_{2}
(see Equation (
14.7
)) shows that
c_{2}(g^{n},x)=\alpha_{2}(g)^{n}
. It follows that
\alpha_{2}(g)^{n}\in C_{H}^{1}
, and Lemma
2.6
,
lemma

2.6
implies that
\alpha_{2}(g)\in C_{H}^{1}
. This proves that
\alpha_{2}(C_{G}^{1})\subseteq C_{H}^{1}
.

Conversely, let us now prove that
C_{H}^{1}\subseteq\alpha_{2}(C_{G}^{1})
. So let
h\in C_{H}^{1}
. By Poincaré recurrence again, for almost every
y\in W
, there exists
m>0
such that
h^{m}\cdot f(y)\in f(W)
. Since the orbits of
C_{G}^{1}
and of
C_{H}^{1}
coincide in restriction to
W\approx f(W)
, we can find
g\in C_{G}^{1}
such that

c_{2}(g,y)=h^{m}
. We deduce that

\alpha_{2}(g)=c^{\prime}_{2}(g,y)=r_{2}\circ c_{2}(g,y)=r_{2}(h^{m})=h^{m}.

Since
\alpha_{2}:(C_{G}^{2})^{\perp}\to(C_{H}^{2})^{\perp}
is an isomorphism, we can also find
g_{0}\in(C_{G}^{2})^{\perp}
such that
\alpha_{2}(g_{0})=h
. Then
\alpha_{2}(g_{0}^{m})=\alpha_{2}(g)
, so
g_{0}^{m}=g
. Lemma
2.6
implies that
g_{0}\in C_{G}^{1}
, and we are done.
∎

The above claim shows that
\alpha_{2}
restricts to an isomorphism from
C_{G}^{1}
to
C_{H}^{1}
. Symmetrically
\alpha_{1}
restricts to an isomorphism from
C_{G}^{2}
to
C_{H}^{2}
.

We now extend
\alpha_{1}
to
P_{G}^{1}
by letting
(\alpha_{1})_{|C_{G}^{1}}=(\alpha_{2})_{|C_{G}^{1}}
. Likewise, we extend
\alpha_{2}
to
P_{G}^{2}
by letting
(\alpha_{2})_{|C_{G}^{2}}=(\alpha_{1})_{|C_{G}^{2}}
. With this extension,
for every
i\in\{1,2\}
, we have

c_{i}(g,x)\in\alpha_{i}(g)C_{H}^{i}\hskip 10.00002pt\forall g\in P_{G}^{i},\ \text{a.e.}\ x\in X.

(14.8)

Indeed,

—

if
g\in C_{G}^{i}
, then
c_{i}(g,x)
belongs to
\bar{\alpha}_{i}(C_{G}^{i})=C_{H}^{i}
, and by our extension of
\alpha_{i}
to
C^{i}_{G}
and
14.14
we get
\alpha_{i}(g)\in C_{H}^{i}
;

—

if
g\in(C_{G}^{i})^{\perp}
, this comes from the fact that
c_{i}(g,x)\in\bar{\alpha}_{i}(gC^{i}_{G})
and the definition of
\alpha_{i}
;

—

in general, every element
g\in P_{G}^{i}
can be decomposed as
g=g_{1}g_{2}
with
g_{1}\in(C_{G}^{i})^{\perp}
and
g_{2}\in C_{G}^{i}
, and Equation (
14.8
) follows from the cocycle relation.

At this point, for every
i\in\{1,2\}
, using Equation (
14.8
) together with the fact that
c
and
c_{i}
are
H
-cohomologous, we have measurable maps
\varphi_{i}:X\to H
and
\kappa_{i}:P_{G}^{i}\times X\to C_{H}^{i}
such that for every
g\in P_{G}^{i}
and a.e.
x\in X
, we have

c(g,x)=\varphi_{i}(g\cdot x)c_{i}(g,x)\varphi_{i}(x)^{-1},

(14.9)

and
c_{i}(g,x)=\alpha_{i}(g)\kappa_{i}(g,x)
, so
eq.

14.6
is verified.

Step 3.
We show that
c_{|C_{G}^{1}\times X}
is
H
-cohomologous to the homomorphism
\alpha_{1}
. This will be done by comparing the two ways of untwisting the cocycle
c
given by Equation (
14.6
), for
i=1
and
i=2
.

Let
X^{*}=\sqcup_{j\in J}X_{j}
be a partition of a conull Borel subset
X^{*}\subseteq X
into at most countably many Borel subsets, such that for every
j\in J
, the maps
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are constant when restricted to
X_{j}
, with respective values denoted by
\gamma_{1,j},\gamma_{2,j}
, and Equation (
14.6
) holds everywhere on
X^{*}
.

Let
h\in C_{H}^{1}
be such that
h
does not belong to any proper parabolic subgroup of
C_{H}^{1}
, and neither do its proper powers (see e.g. the proof of
lemma

2.19
for the existence of
h
). Let
g\in C_{G}^{1}
such that
\alpha_{1}(g)=\alpha_{2}(g)=h
. For every
j\in J
, let
x\in X_{j}
and
k_{j}>0
be such that
g^{k_{j}}\cdot x\in X_{j}
(these exist by Poincaré recurrence). Since
C_{G}^{1}\subseteq P_{G}^{1}\cap P_{G}^{2}
, we can use Equation (
14.6
) with both
i=1
and
i=2
to write

c(g^{k_{j}},x)=\gamma_{1,j}\alpha_{1}(g)^{k_{j}}\kappa_{1}(g^{k_{j}},x)\gamma_{1,j}^{-1}=\gamma_{2,j}\alpha_{2}(g)^{k_{j}}\kappa_{2}(g^{k_{j}},x)\gamma_{2,j}^{-1}.

(14.10)

Let
r_{2}:H\to C_{H}^{2}
be the natural retraction (see the last paragraph of Section
2.2.1
,
section

2.2.1
). Since
C_{H}^{1}\subseteq(C_{H}^{2})^{\perp}
(by Step
1
), we have
r_{2}(C_{H}^{1})=\{1\}
. In particular remark that
\alpha_{1}(g),\alpha_{2}(g),\kappa_{1}(g^{k_{j}},x)\in C^{1}_{H}
. By applying
r_{2}
to the rightmost equality of Equation (
14.10
), we deduce that
\kappa_{2}(g^{k_{j}},x)=1
for every
x\in X^{*}
.

Let
\beta_{j}=\gamma_{2,j}^{-1}\gamma_{1,j}
. Using that
\kappa_{2}(g^{k_{j}},x)=1
, Equation (
14.10
) rewrites as

\beta_{j}\alpha_{1}(g)^{k_{j}}\kappa_{1}(g^{k_{j}},x)\beta_{j}^{-1}=\alpha_{2}(g)^{k_{j}}.

Therefore
\beta_{j}C_{H}^{1}\beta_{j}^{-1}\cap C_{H}^{1}
contains
\alpha_{2}(g)^{k_{j}}
, so our choice of
g
ensures that
\beta_{j}C_{H}^{1}\beta_{j}^{-1}\cap C_{H}^{1}
cannot be a proper parabolic subgroup of
C_{H}^{1}
. It follows that
\beta_{j}C_{H}^{1}\beta_{j}^{-1}\supseteq C_{H}^{1}
. The second point of Proposition
2.3
implies that in fact
\beta_{j}C_{H}^{1}\beta_{j}^{-1}=C_{H}^{1}
, i.e.
\beta_{j}
belongs to the normalizer of
C_{H}^{1}
.

In other words, for every
x\in X_{j}
, the constant value of
\varphi_{2}(x)^{-1}\varphi_{1}(x)
belongs to
C_{H}^{1}\times(C_{H}^{1})^{\perp}
. Since this is true for every
j\in J
, there exist measurable maps
\eta_{1}:X\to C_{H}^{1}
and
\mu_{1}:X\to(C_{H}^{1})^{\perp}
such that for every
x\in X^{*}
, one has
\varphi_{2}(x)=\varphi_{1}(x)\eta_{1}(x)\mu_{1}(x)
. Now, by rewriting Equation (
14.6
) with
i=2
, we deduce that for every
x\in X^{*}
and every
g\in P_{G}^{1}
, one has

c(g,x)=\varphi_{1}(g\cdot x)\eta_{1}(g\cdot x)\mu_{1}(g\cdot x)\alpha_{2}(g)\kappa_{2}(g,x)\mu_{1}(x)^{-1}\eta_{1}(x)^{-1}\varphi_{1}(x)^{-1},

in other words

\varphi_{1}(g\cdot x)^{-1}c(g,x)\varphi_{1}(x)=\eta_{1}(g\cdot x)\mu_{1}(g\cdot x)\alpha_{2}(g)\kappa_{2}(g,x)\mu_{1}(x)^{-1}\eta_{1}(x)^{-1}.

(14.11)

Now, when
g\in C_{G}^{1}
, using Equations (
14.8
) and (
14.9
), the leftmost term belongs to
C_{H}^{1}
. We now apply the retraction
r_{1}:H\to C_{H}^{1}
(which sends
(C_{H}^{1})^{\perp}
to
\{1\}
, so in particular sends
C_{H}^{2}
to
\{1\}
), to Equation (
14.11
). We deduce that

\varphi_{1}(g\cdot x)^{-1}c(g,x)\varphi_{1}(x)=\eta_{1}(g\cdot x)\alpha_{2}(g)\eta_{1}(x)^{-1},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall g\in C_{G}^{1},\penalty\ \text{a.e.}\penalty\ x\in X.

This proves that there exists a measurable map
\psi:X\to H
and a homomorphism
\alpha_{1}:C_{G}^{1}\to H
such that for every
g\in C_{G}^{1}
and a.e.
x\in X
, one has

c(g,x)=\psi(g\cdot x)\alpha_{1}(g)\psi(x)^{-1}.

In other words, we have untwisted the cocycle
c
to a group homomorphism on some vertex group of
G
. An application of Lemma
14.8
then concludes our proof of Theorem
14.2
.
∎

14.3
An application to von Neumann algebras

When combined with a uniqueness theorem for Cartan subalgebras established by Chifan and Kunnawalkam Elayavalli in the framework of Popa’s deformation/rigidity theory, Theorem
14.2
has the following consequence at the level of von Neumann algebras. We refer to
section

5.4.2
,
section

5.4.2
for the needed definitions.

Corollary 14.15
.

Let
\Gamma_{G},\Gamma_{H}
be two connected finite simple graphs, with no vertex joined to every other vertex. Let
G,H
be graph products with countably infinite torsion-free vertex groups over
\Gamma_{G},\Gamma_{H}
, respectively. Let
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
be two free, vertexwise ergodic, measure-preserving actions on standard probability spaces. Assume that either

—

G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise weakly mixing, or

—

G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise totally ergodic, or

—

H
has the unique root property.

If the
\mathrm{II}_{1}
factors
L(G\curvearrowright X)
and
L(H\curvearrowright Y)
are isomorphic (or more generally, if
L(H\curvearrowright Y)
is isomorphic to an amplification
L(G\curvearrowright X)^{t}
with
t\in(0,1]
), then the two actions are conjugate.

Proof.

By
[
CKE21
, Theorem 1.3]
, the subalgebra
L^{\infty}(Y)
is, up to unitary conjugation, the unique Cartan subalgebra of
L(H\curvearrowright Y)
. Let
U\subseteq X
be a Borel subset of measure
t
, and let
\mathcal{R}
be the restriction to
U
of the orbit equivalence relation for the
G
-action on
X
. Then
L(G\curvearrowright X)^{t}\simeq L(\mathcal{R})
, and
L^{\infty}(U)
is a Cartan subalgebra. So there exists an isomorphism
\theta:L(H\curvearrowright Y)\to L(G\curvearrowright X)^{t}
sending
L^{\infty}(Y)
to
L^{\infty}(U)
. By
[
Sin55
,
FM77
]
, this implies that the orbit equivalence relation of the
H
-action on
Y
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{R}
. In particular, there exists a stable orbit equivalence with compression
\frac{1}{t}\geq 1
from
G\curvearrowright X
to
H\curvearrowright Y
. Theorem
14.2
thus implies that the two actions are conjugate.
∎

Remark 14.16
.

As in Corollary
5.15
, we could also derive the triviality of the fundamental group of any action
G\curvearrowright X
which is either vertexwise weakly mixing (or vertexwise totally ergodic), with
G
torsion-free, or vertexwise ergodic, with
G
having the unique root property. Likewise, the associated von Neumann algebra
L(G\curvearrowright X)
has trivial fundamental group.

15
Superrigidity

The goal of this section is to show the following theorem.

Theorem 15.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a connected finite simple graph, with no vertex joined to every other vertex. Let
G
be a graph product with countably infinite vertex groups over
\Gamma
, satisfying the unique root property, and let
H
be an arbitrary countable group. Let
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
be two free, ergodic, measure-preserving actions on standard probability spaces. Assume that

—

one of the following two options holds:

—

either
G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise ergodic, and
G
has the unique root property,

—

or
G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise totally ergodic, and
G
is torsion-free,

—

and
H\curvearrowright Y
is mildly mixing.

If the actions
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
are stably orbit equivalent, then they are virtually conjugate.

Recall that a group is
ICC
(standing for
infinite conjugacy classes
) if the conjugacy class of every non-trivial element of
G
is infinite. We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 15.2
.

If
G
is a graph product over a finite simple graph
\Gamma
such that no vertex is joined to every other vertex, then
G
is ICC.

Proof.

Write
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{k}
, where each
\Gamma_{j}
is irreducible (possibly
k=1
). It is enough to prove that each
G_{\Gamma_{j}}
is ICC. So let
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. Our assumption that
\Gamma
is not equal to the star of one of its vertices implies that
\Gamma_{j}
is not a clique. By
[
MO15
, Corollary 2.13]
, the group
G_{\Gamma_{j}}
is acylindrically hyperbolic (equivalently by
[
Osi16
, Theorem 1.2]
, the group
G
contains a proper infinite hyperbolically embedded subgroup in the sense of
[
DGO17
, Definition 2.1]
). In addition, by
[
MV22
, Corollary 2.9]
, the group
G_{\Gamma_{j}}
has no non-trivial finite normal subgroup. So by
[
DGO17
, Theorem 8.14]
, the group
G_{\Gamma_{j}}
is ICC. So
G
is ICC, as desired.
∎

We are now in position to prove Theorem
15.1
.

Proof of Theorem
15.1
.

We first assume that the action
G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise ergodic and
G
has the unique root property. In the terminology from
[
HHI23
, Section 7]
, vertexwise ergodicity of
G\curvearrowright X
is exactly
\mathcal{F}
-ergodicity, with
\mathcal{F}
the collection of all vertex groups of
G
. And Theorem
14.2
shows that
(G,\mathcal{F})
is strongly cocycle rigid in the sense of
[
HHI23
, Section 7]
. Since
G
is ICC (Lemma
15.2
), the conclusion therefore follows from
[
HHI23
, Theorem 7.1]
.

We now assume that the action
G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise totally ergodic and
G
is torsion-free. In the terminology from
[
HHI23
, Section 7]
, vertexwise total ergodicity is exactly
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-ergodicity, with
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
the collection of all infinite subgroups of vertex groups of
G
. As above, Theorem
14.2
shows that
(G,\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
is strongly cocycle rigid in the sense of
[
HHI23
, Section 7]
, and the conclusion follows from
[
HHI23
, Theorem 7.1]
.
∎

Remark 15.3
.

Again, using the uniqueness of the Cartan subalgebra, up to unitary conjugacy, for
L(G\curvearrowright X)
, Theorem
15.1
can be reformulated as a
W^{*}
-rigidity statement.

Let us finally mention the following corollary to Theorem
15.1
, which states the rigidity of graph product actions within the class of mixing group actions.

Corollary 15.4

(
1.14
)
.

Let
G,H
be two torsion-free countable groups. Let
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
be two mixing measure-preserving actions on standard probability spaces. Assume that
G
splits as a graph product over a connected finite simple graph
\Gamma
, with no vertex joined to every other vertex.

If the two actions
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
are orbit equivalent, then they are conjugate.

Proof.

Being mixing, the two actions are in particular totally ergodic (and in particular the action
G\curvearrowright X
is vertexwise totally ergodic) and mildly mixing. They are also essentially free by
[
TD15
, Theorem 1.4]
. Therefore Theorem
15.1
applies and shows that the two actions are virtually conjugate. But
G
and
H
have no finite normal subgroup, and an induced action of the form
\mathrm{Ind}_{G^{\prime}}^{G}(X)
is never mixing if
G^{\prime}\subsetneq G
(because
G^{\prime}
does not act ergodically on
\mathrm{Ind}_{G^{\prime}}^{G}(X)
). Therefore the actions
G\curvearrowright X
and
H\curvearrowright Y
are conjugate.
∎

Part V
Measure equivalence rigidity of some graph products

This chapter deals with measure equivalence rigidity of graph products. Contrary to the previous chapter, we will not make any ergodicity assumption on the couplings we consider; but on the other hand, we will impose some strong rigidity conditions on the vertex groups.

A general question is the following: to what extent is it true that if the vertex groups are rigid in measure equivalence, then the graph product is rigid in measure equivalence?

We will not answer this question in full generality, but we will give a sufficient criterion in Section
16
, and an example in Section
17
. Our example is, to our knowledge, the first example of a group which is rigid in measure equivalence, but not in quasi-isometry, among torsion-free groups. On this matter, let us make two remarks.

—

It is not true that a graph product of quasi-isometrically rigid groups is quasi-isometrically rigid. A basic observation is that in a graph product
G
, changing a vertex group
G_{v}
by
G_{v}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
can often yield a group which is quasi-isometric but not commensurable to
G
. So the phenomena established in this chapter are radically different from their counterparts in quasi-isometry.

—

In the converse direction, examples of groups that are rigid in quasi-isometry but not in measure equivalence exist. First, some amenable groups are quasi-isometrically rigid, while countably infinite amenable groups are all measure equivalent by the Ornstein–Weiss theorem
[
OW80
]
. Quasi-isometrically rigid amenable groups include
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
(Pansu
[
Pan83
]
) or solvable Baumslag–Solitar groups (Farb–Mosher
[
FM99
]
). Examples of non-amenable groups that are rigid in quasi-isometry but not in measure equivalence include many right-angled Artin groups, see work of Huang
[
Hua18
]
on the quasi-isometry side.

When dealing with the above question, it is in fact useful to impose a very strong form of rigidity on the vertex groups
G_{v}
, and require at least that for every
v\in V\Gamma
, every self measure equivalence coupling of
G_{v}
factors through the tautological coupling on
G_{v}
itself, by left-right multiplication. This will be an important assumption in our criterion in Section
16
.

To illustrate the subtleness of the question, let us make the following observation in the context of
L^{1}
-measure equivalence: it is possible to have a graph product
G
of countably infinite groups over a finite simple graph with no transvection and no partial conjugation (and not reduced to a point), where all vertex groups are rigid in
L^{1}
-measure equivalence in the sense that every countable group that is
L^{1}
-measure equivalent to
G_{v}
is virtually isomorphic to
G_{v}
, but
G
is not. Indeed, it is enough to take the vertex groups to be isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
, and observe that some right-angled Artin groups are not rigid in
L^{1}
-measure equivalence
[
HH23
]
, while
\mathbb{Z}
is
[
Aus16
, Corollary 1.2]
.

16
An ME-rigidity criterion for graph products

The goal of the present section is to prove the following theorem. The definition of Monod–Shalom’s class
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{reg}}
is given just below the statement.

Theorem 16.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite simple graph with no transvection and no partial conjugation, with
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)=\{1\}
. For every
v\in V\Gamma
, let
G_{v}
be a countably infinite group, and let
G
be the graph product over
\Gamma
with vertex groups
(G_{v})_{v\in V\Gamma}
. Assume that the following four hypotheses hold.

(\mathrm{H}_{1})

For every vertex
v\in V\Gamma
, there exists an extension
\hat{G}_{v}=G_{v}\rtimes F_{v}
with
F_{v}
finite, such that for every self measure equivalence coupling
\Omega_{v}
of
G_{v}
, there exists a Borel
(G_{v}\times G_{v})
-equivariant map
\Omega_{v}\to\hat{G}_{v}
.

(\mathrm{H}_{2})

For every vertex
v\in V\Gamma
, the group
G_{v}
is torsion-free, has no proper finite-index subgroup, and belongs to the class
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{reg}}
.

(\mathrm{H}_{3})

The vertex groups are pairwise not measure equivalent.

(\mathrm{H}_{4})

For every clique
C
with
VC=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
, the image of every injective homomorphism
G_{v_{1}}\to G_{v_{1}}\times\dots\times G_{v_{k}}
is equal to
G_{v_{1}}\times\{1\}\times\dots\times\{1\}
.

Then every torsion-free countable group which is measure equivalent to
G
, is in fact isomorphic to
G
.

As usual, throughout the section, all the stated equivariances are valid almost everywhere.

The class
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{reg}}
, introduced by Monod–Shalom in
[
MS03
]
, is the class of all countable groups
G
such that
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(G,\ell^{2}(G))\neq\{0\}
. Of particular importance for us is the following example.

Example 16.2
.

Recall that a group is
acylindrically hyperbolic
in the sense of Osin
[
Osi16
]
if it admits a non-elementary acylindrical action on a hyperbolic space (see also Appendix
A.2
,
section

A.2
). By a theorem of Hamenstädt
[
Ham08
, Theorem A]
, all acylindrically hyperbolic groups belong to the class
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{reg}}
.

Remark 16.3
.

Hypothesis
(\mathrm{H}_{2})
implies in particular that all vertex groups are ICC (i.e. every non-trivial conjugacy class in
G_{v}
is infinite), since a countable torsion-free
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{reg}}
group is always ICC by
[
MS06
, Proposition 7.11]
.

Outline of the proof, and organization of the section.

Recall that the group
G
acts on its right-angled building
\mathbb{D}_{G}
(see Section
2.3.1
,
section

2.3.1
for its definition). We first prove that the
G
-stabilizer
G_{u}
of every vertex
u\in V\mathbb{D}_{G}
is rigid in measure equivalence in a strong sense: every self-coupling of
G_{u}
factors through the action on (some finite-index extension of)
G_{u}
by left-right multiplication. When
u
is a rank
1
vertex, the group
G_{u}
is conjugate to a vertex group for the graph product structure of
G
, and rigidity is exactly the contents of Hypothesis
(\mathrm{H}_{1})
. Stabilizers of higher rank vertices split as direct products of stabilizers of rank
1
vertices, and we exploit an argument from the successive works of Monod–Shalom
[
MS06
]
and Kida
[
Kid10
]
to establish the measure equivalence rigidity of these direct products. This step is carried in Section
16.1
.

Let now
H
be a torsion-free group which is measure equivalent to
G
. We know from Part
II
of the present work that every self-coupling of
G
factors through
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
. By a standard technique, originating from the work of Furman
[
Fur99a
]
, we can transfer the rigidity from self-couplings to arbitrary couplings, and deduce that
H
also acts on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
, and that (through this action) every measure equivalence coupling
\Omega
between
G
and
H
factors through
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
. This is proved in Section
5.2
. A key technical ingredient in the proof is to establish a strong ICC property for the Polish group
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
– our proof of this fact relies on previous work of Huang and the second-named author
[
HH23
]
.

In Section
16.3
, we then analyze this
H
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
. In a way that is similar to our work in Part
II
, we prove that for every vertex
u\in V\mathbb{D}_{G}
, the coupling
\Omega
(between
G
and
H
) restricts to a measure equivalence coupling between
G_{u}
and
H_{u}
. And the combination of Hypotheses
(\mathrm{H}_{1})
and
(\mathrm{H}_{2})
then forces
H_{u}
to be isomorphic to
G_{u}
(the facts that
H_{u}
is torsion-free and that
G_{u}
has no proper finite-index subgroup are used to obtain an actual isomorphism, not a virtual one). In other words
H
acts on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
with the same vertex stabilizers as
G
. And, by crucially using our assumption that all groups considered have no proper finite-index subgroup, we also prove in Section
16.3.3
that the
H
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
shares a common fundamental domain (which is a subcomplex) with the action of
G
.

In the terminology of Bridson–Haefliger
[
BH99
]
, this says that
H
has the structure of a simple complex of groups over the fundamental domain
Y
for the
G
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
. In other words
H
is exactly the group which is obtained from the free product of all
H
-stabilizers of cells in
Y
, by taking the quotient by the relations coming from the natural inclusions of stabilizers (coming from face inclusions of the cells). Recall that each cell stabilizer splits as a direct product of some of the vertex groups
G_{v}
, and there remains to understand the inclusion homomorphisms. This is where we crucially use our Hypothesis
(\mathrm{H}_{4})
: it forces all inclusion homomorphisms to be factor inclusions. In other words
H
has exactly the presentation of a graph product, and therefore
H
is isomorphic to
G
.

16.1
Self-couplings of clique subgroups

Using the fact that all vertex groups belong to the class
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{reg}}
, we extend Assumption
(\mathrm{H}_{1})
to all self-couplings of clique subgroups of
G
(and not just to self-couplings of vertex groups). This is the contents of the following proposition, which was essentially established by Kida
[
Kid10
, Section 7]
, relying on work of Monod–Shalom
[
MS06
]
. We provide a brief argument as Kida’s statements were formulated in the setting of mapping class groups.

Proposition 16.4
.

Let
G_{1},\dots,G_{n}
be torsion-free countable groups in the class
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{reg}}
, and for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, let
\hat{G}_{i}
be a finite-index extension of
G_{i}
. Let
G=G_{1}\times\dots\times G_{n}
, and let
\hat{G}=\hat{G}_{1}\times\dots\times\hat{G}_{n}
. Assume that

—

for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, and every self measure equivalence coupling
\Omega_{i}
of
G_{i}
, there exists a
(G_{i}\times G_{i})
-equivariant Borel map
\Omega_{i}\to\hat{G}_{i}
, and

—

for any distinct
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, the groups
G_{i}
and
G_{j}
are not measure equivalent.

Then for every self measure equivalence coupling
\Omega
of
G
, there exists a
(G\times G)
-equivariant Borel map
\Omega\to\hat{G}
.

Proof.

By considering the ergodic decomposition, every self measure equivalence coupling of
G
can be disintegrated into ergodic self measure equivalence couplings, see the second point of
[
Fur99a
, Lemma 2.2]
. Therefore, in view of
[
FMW04
, Corollary 5.4]
(applied to
G=H
, with
\Omega
in place of their space
X
, and
\hat{G}
in place of their space
Y
), it is enough to treat the case where the action of
G\times G
on
\Omega
is ergodic. From now on we work under this extra assumption.

Using an argument from the proof of
[
MS06
, Theorem 1.16]
, Kida proved in
[
Kid10
, Section 7, p. 1895]
that for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, there exists a standard measure space
\Omega_{i}
, equipped with

—

a measure-preserving action of
G_{i}\times G_{i}
;

—

a measurable projection
p_{i}:\Omega\to\Omega_{i}
which is both
(G_{i}\times G_{i})
-equivariant, and
(G_{j}\times G_{j})
-invariant for every
j\neq i
;

—

a
(G_{i}\times G_{i})
-invariant Borel partition
\Omega_{i}^{*}=\sqcup_{k}\Omega_{i,k}
of a
(G_{i}\times G_{i})
-invariant conull Borel subset
\Omega_{i}^{*}
into at most countably many subspaces
\Omega_{i,k}
, such that each
\Omega_{i,k}
is a self measure equivalence coupling of
G_{i}
.

(Notice that Kida has a space
\Omega_{i}
which is invariant under
G_{i}\times G_{t(i)}
for some bijection
t:\{1,\dots,n\}\to\{1,\dots,n\}
, but our second assumption implies that
t(i)=i
.)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\theta_{i,k}:\Omega_{i,k}\to\hat{G}_{i}
be a
(G_{i}\times G_{i})
-equivariant Borel map, given by our first assumption. Combining these maps as
k
varies in
\mathbb{N}
yields a
(G_{i}\times G_{i})
-equivariant Borel map
\theta_{i}:\Omega_{i}\to\hat{G}_{i}
. Then the map
(\theta_{1}\circ p_{1},\dots,\theta_{n}\circ p_{n}):\Omega\to\hat{G}
gives the desired almost
(G\times G)
-equivariant Borel map.
∎

16.2
Factoring the coupling through the right-angled building

The goal of this section is to prove the following proposition (we refer to Section
2.3.1
,
section

2.3.1
, for the definition of the right-angled building
\mathbb{D}_{G}
).

Proposition 16.5
.

Let
G
be a graph product with countably infinite vertex groups, over a finite simple graph
\Gamma
with no transvection and no partial conjugation. Let
H
be a countable group, and let
\Omega
be a measure equivalence coupling between
G
and
H
. Let
\mathbb{D}_{G}
be the right-angled building of
G
.

Then there exist a homomorphism
\iota:H\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
and a
(G\times H)
-equivariant Borel map
\theta:\Omega\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
, where the action of
G\times H
on
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
is via
(g,h)\cdot f=gf\iota(h)^{-1}
.

We already know that self-couplings of
G
factor through
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
(Corollary
5.6
,
5.6
). Our proof relies on a general strategy to transfer this fact to an information about arbitrary couplings between
G
and
H
. This strategy was initiated by Furman in
[
Fur99a
]
, and we will use a version found in
[
Kid11
]
(or alternatively
[
BFS13
]
). This argument requires establishing a strong ICC property for the Polish group
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
, which is the contents of our next lemma.

Lemma 16.6
.

Let
G
be a graph product of countably infinite groups over a finite simple graph
\Gamma
, such that no vertex
v\in V\Gamma
satisfies
\Gamma=\mathrm{star}(v)
. Let
\mathbb{D}_{G}
be its right-angled building.

Then the only Borel probability measure on
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
which is invariant by the conjugation by any element of
G
is the Dirac mass at the identity.

The conclusion of the lemma precisely says that
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
is
strongly ICC
relative to
G
in the sense of Bader–Furman–Sauer
[
BFS13
, Definition 2.2]
(or that the inclusion
G\subseteq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
is
strongly ICC
in the terminology of
[
HH23
, Section 4.2]
).

Proof.

This was proved when
G
is the right-angled Artin group
A_{\Gamma}
over
\Gamma
in
[
HH23
, Proposition 5.1]
. We now explain how to reduce the proof to the case of a right-angled Artin group. We will use the language of
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
cube complexes and their half-spaces, for which we refer to
[
Sag14
]
. We will also need the notion of the
regular boundary
of a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
cube complex
X
, a natural compact subspace of its Roller boundary equal to the closure of the set of regular points in the sense of Fernós in
[
Fer18
, Definition 7.3]
, also denoted
S(X)
in
[
KS16
]
. All the necessary background for this proof was also reviewed in
[
HH23
, Section 5.2]
.

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\Gamma}
be the right-angled building of the right-angled Artin group over
\Gamma
. Recall from Lemma
2.13
,
2.13
, that there exists an isomorphism from
\mathbb{D}_{G}
to
\mathbb{D}_{\Gamma}
that sends
G
-orbits to
A_{\Gamma}
-orbits.

Step 1.
Assuming that
\Gamma
is irreducible (and not reduced to one vertex), we will prove that the
G
-action on the regular boundary
R(\mathbb{D}_{G})
is minimal and strongly proximal in the sense of Furstenberg
[
Fur63
]
, i.e. it is minimal and the
G
-orbit of any probability measure on
R(\mathbb{D}_{G})
contains a Dirac mass in its weak-
*
closure.

By
[
HH23
, Lemma 5.3]
, the building
\mathbb{D}_{G}\approx\mathbb{D}_{\Gamma}
is irreducible (it does not split as a product), and

—

A_{\Gamma}
acts non-elementarily, i.e. with no global fixed point, and no finite orbit in the visual boundary
\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{D}_{\Gamma}
, and

—

A_{\Gamma}
acts essentially, in the sense of Caprace–Sageev
[
CS11
]
, i.e. no
A_{\Gamma}
-orbit stays in a bounded neighborhood of a half-space of
\mathbb{D}_{\Gamma}
.

The first of these properties (non-elementarity) was in fact proved by Caprace–Hume
[
CH15
, Section 6.2]
in the more general context of graph products, so it applies to the
G
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
. The second property (essentiality) for the
G
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
follows from the same property for the
A_{\Gamma}
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{\Gamma}
as these two actions have the same orbits.

It thus follows from
[
KS16
, Proposition 1]
that the
G
-action on the regular boundary
R(\mathbb{D}_{G})
is minimal and strongly proximal, as claimed.

Step 2.
We now conclude the proof of the lemma. Write
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{k}
as a join, where each
\Gamma_{j}
is irreducible (possibly with
k=1
). Notice that no
\Gamma_{j}
is reduced to a vertex, by our assumption that
\Gamma
is not contained in the star of a vertex. Let
G=G_{1}\times\dots\times G_{k}
be the corresponding direct product decomposition. For every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, let
\mathbb{D}_{j}
be the right-angled building of
G_{j}
, and let
R=R(\mathbb{D}_{1})\times\dots\times R(\mathbb{D}_{k})
. By Step 1, for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, the action of
G_{j}
on
R(\mathbb{D}_{j})
is minimal and strongly proximal, and it is faithful in view of
[
HH23
, Lemma 5.6]
. Let now
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
which is invariant under conjugation by every element of
G
. By
[
HH23
, Lemma 5.6]
, we can pushforward
\mu
to a probability measure
\bar{\mu}
on
\mathrm{Homeo}(R)
which is invariant under the conjugation by every element of
G
. It thus follows from
[
HH23
, Lemma 5.2]
that
\bar{\mu}
is the Dirac mass at the identity. Using again that the map
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})\to\mathrm{Homeo}(R)
is injective (
[
HH23
, Lemma 5.6]
), it follows that
\mu
is the Dirac mass at the identity.
∎

We are now in position to prove Proposition
16.5
.

Proof of Proposition
16.5
.

Let
\iota_{G}:G\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
be the natural inclusion. By Corollary
5.6
,
5.6
, for every self measure equivalence coupling
\Sigma
of
G
, there exists a
(G\times G)
-equivariant Borel map
\Sigma\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
. Combined with Lemma
16.6
, this precisely says that
G
is coupling rigid with respect to
(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G}),\iota_{G})
in the sense of Kida
[
Kid11
, Definition 3.3]
.
The conclusion thus follows from
[
Kid11
, Theorem 3.5]
.
∎

16.3
Properties of the
H
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}

In the whole section, we will work in the following setting.

General setting.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite simple graph with no transvection and no partial conjugation, with
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)=\{1\}
, and
G
be a graph product with countably infinite vertex groups over
\Gamma
that satisfies Assumptions
(\mathrm{H}_{1})
,
(\mathrm{H}_{2})
and
(\mathrm{H}_{3})
from Theorem
16.1
(Assumption
(\mathrm{H}_{4})
will only be used in Section
16.4
). Let
H
be a torsion-free countable group which is measure equivalent to
G
, and let
(\Omega,m)
be a measure equivalence coupling between
G
and
H
. Proposition
16.5
gives us

—

a homomorphism
\iota:H\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
;

—

a
(G\times H)
-equivariant Borel map
\theta:\Omega\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
.

In particular
\iota
gives an action of
H
on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
, and from now on we will work with this action of
H
.

16.3.1
The
H
-action is type preserving

Recall from Section
2.3.1
that vertices of
\mathbb{D}_{G}
correspond to left cosets of the form
gG_{\Upsilon}
, for some
g\in G
and some clique subgraph
\Upsilon\subseteq\Gamma
. The rank of a vertex is the cardinality of
V\Upsilon
. We also define the
type
of a vertex of
\mathbb{D}_{G}
as the corresponding subgraph
\Upsilon
. More generally, given a cube
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{G}
, we define the
type
of
\sigma
as the set consisting of all types of vertices of
\sigma
. Notice that the type of
\sigma
is determined by the type of its smallest and largest vertices, for the partial order given by inclusion (when viewing vertices as cosets in
G
).

The fact that
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)=\{1\}
will be used in the form of the following lemma.

Lemma 16.7
.

The actions of
G
and of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
have the same orbits of cubes. In particular, the action of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
preserves types of cubes.

Proof.

Since the
G
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
preserves types of vertices, it preserves types of cubes, and therefore it is enough to prove that
G
and
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
have the same orbits of cubes.

Step 1:
The actions of
G
and
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
have the same orbits of vertices.

When
G=A_{\Gamma}
is the right-angled Artin group over
\Gamma
, this was proved in
[
HH23
, Corollary 6.3]
: indeed the group
\hat{G}
appearing in
[
HH23
, Corollary 6.3]
splits as a semi-direct product
G\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
(see its definition in the first paragraph of
[
HH23
, Section 6.1]
), so it coincides with
G
under our assumption that
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)=\{1\}
.

The general case now follows from the fact that there always exists an isomorphism
\mathbb{D}_{G}\to\mathbb{D}_{\Gamma}
(where
\mathbb{D}_{\Gamma}
is the right-angled building for
A_{\Gamma}
), sending
G
-orbits of vertices to
A_{\Gamma}
-orbits of vertices (Lemma
2.13
,
2.13
).

Step 2:
The actions of
G
and
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
have the same orbits of cubes.

Let
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{G}
be two cubes in the same
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
-orbit; we aim to prove that they are in fact in the same
G
-orbit. Let
v_{\min},v_{\max}
be the minimal and maximal vertices of
\sigma
, and let
v^{\prime}_{\min},v^{\prime}_{\max}
be the minimal and maximal vertices of
\sigma^{\prime}
. Since
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
preserves types of vertices (as a consequence of Step 1), the vertices
v_{\min}
and
v^{\prime}_{\min}
are in the same
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
-orbit. Using Step 1 again, they are in fact in the same
G
-orbit: there exists
g\in G
such that
v^{\prime}_{\min}=gv_{\min}
. Likewise, the vertices
v_{\max}
and
v^{\prime}_{\max}
are in the same
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
-orbit. In particular they have the same type, which we denote by
\Lambda
(a subgraph of
\Gamma
). So
v_{\max}
(resp.
v^{\prime}_{\max}
) is the unique coset of
G_{\Lambda}
that contains the coset of
G
corresponding to
v_{\min}
(resp. to
v^{\prime}_{\min}=gv_{\min}
). Therefore
v^{\prime}_{\max}=gv_{\max}
, and this in enough to ensure that
\sigma^{\prime}=g\sigma
.
∎

Let us record the following consequence of Lemma
16.7
.

Corollary 16.8
.

Let
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{G}
be a cube, and let
v
be the unique vertex of
\sigma
of minimal rank. Then the stabilizers of
v
and of
\sigma
in
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
coincide.

Proof.

Since
v
is the unique vertex of
\sigma
of its type and the action of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
preserves types of vertices (Lemma
16.7
), we have
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})}(v)
.

The converse inclusion follows from the observation that
\sigma
is the unique cube of its type that contains
v
as its minimal vertex. Since
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
preserves types of cubes (Lemma
16.7
), this implies that the
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
-stabilizer of
v
also preserves
\sigma
.
∎

16.3.2
Stabilizers for the actions of
G
and
H
are measure equivalent

Given a cube
\sigma\subseteq V\mathbb{D}_{G}
, we let
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\sigma),\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})}(\sigma)
be the respective stabilizers of
\sigma
for the actions of
G
, of
H
, and of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
. We let
\Omega_{\sigma}:=\theta^{-1}(\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})}(\sigma))
, where we recall that
\theta:\Omega\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
is our given
(G\times H)
-equivariant Borel map. Then
\Omega_{\sigma}
is a Borel subset of
\Omega
which is
(\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\sigma)\times\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma))
-invariant. The following lemma is a special case of
[
HH23
, Corollary 4.4]
.

Lemma 16.9
.

For every cube
\sigma\subseteq V\mathbb{D}_{G}
, the space
\Omega_{\sigma}
is a measure equivalence coupling between
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\sigma)
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

In view of Corollary
16.8
, it is enough to prove the lemma when
\sigma
is a vertex, so we will assume so.

By Lemma
16.7
, the groups
G
and
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
have the same orbits of vertices for their actions on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
. The lemma therefore follows from
[
HH23
, Corollary 4.4]
, applied with
K=\mathbb{D}_{G}
, with
L=\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
, with
\mathsf{G}=G
, and with
\mathsf{H}=H
.
∎

As a particular case, we obtain the following statement.

Corollary 16.10
.

The
H
-stabilizer of every rank
0
vertex of
\mathbb{D}_{G}
is trivial.

Proof.

Let
v\in V\mathbb{D}_{G}
be a rank
0
vertex. By Lemma
16.9
, the stabilizer
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v)
is measure equivalent to
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)
, which is trivial. Therefore
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v)
is finite, and in fact trivial because
H
is torsion-free.
∎

In fact we will deduce more generally that
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\sigma)
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
are isomorphic for every cube
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{G}
, in view of the following lemma.

Lemma 16.11
.

Let
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{G}
be a cube. Let
K
be a torsion-free countable group which is measure equivalent to
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\sigma)
.

Then
K
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

The group
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\sigma)
is isomorphic to
G_{\Upsilon}=G_{v_{1}}\times\dots\times G_{v_{k}}
for some clique
\Upsilon
of
\Gamma
with
V\Upsilon=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
. We also write
\hat{G}_{\Upsilon}:=\hat{G}_{v_{1}}\times\dots\times\hat{G}_{v_{k}}
. Recall that each
G_{v_{i}}
is ICC (Remark
16.3
), in particular
G_{\Upsilon}
is ICC.

Hypotheses
(\mathrm{H}_{1})
,

(\mathrm{H}_{2})
and
(\mathrm{H}_{3})
, together with Proposition
16.4
, ensure that for every self measure equivalence coupling
\Omega_{\Upsilon}
of
G_{\Upsilon}
, there exists a
(G_{\Upsilon}\times G_{\Upsilon})
-Borel map
\Omega_{\Upsilon}\to\hat{G}_{\Upsilon}
. Therefore
[
Kid10
, Theorem 6.1]
implies that there exists a homomorphism
\pi_{\sigma}:K\to\hat{G}_{\Upsilon}
with finite kernel, and finite-index image. Since
K
is torsion-free,
\pi_{\sigma}
is in fact injective. Then
\pi_{\sigma}(K)\cap G_{\Upsilon}
is a finite-index subgroup of
G_{\Upsilon}
, and is therefore equal to
G_{\Upsilon}
in view of Hypothesis
(\mathrm{H}_{2})
. Recall also from Hypothesis
(\mathrm{H}_{1})
that for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we have a semi-direct product
\hat{G}_{v_{i}}=G_{v_{i}}\rtimes F_{v_{i}}
, with
F_{v_{i}}
finite. Then the image of
\pi_{\sigma}(K)
in each
F_{v_{i}}
is trivial: otherwise
\pi_{\sigma}(K)
would intersect a lift of
F_{v_{1}}\times\dots\times F_{v_{k}}
to
\hat{G}_{\Upsilon}
non-trivially, contradicting that
K
is torsion-free. Therefore
\pi_{\sigma}(K)=G_{\Upsilon}
, which shows that
K
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\sigma)
.
∎

16.3.3
A common strict fundamental domain for the actions of
G
and
H

Let
Y_{G}\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{G}
be the standard fundamental domain for
G
, spanned by all vertices of the form
G_{\Upsilon}
, where
\Upsilon\subseteq\Gamma
is a clique. Notice that
Y_{G}
is a
strict fundamental domain
for the
G
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
, i.e. it is a subcomplex that meets every
G
-orbit in exactly one point. We will prove in Lemma
16.13
below that
Y_{G}
is also a strict fundamental domain for the
H
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
. We start with a lemma.

Lemma 16.12
.

For every rank
1
vertex
v\in VY_{G}
, every measure equivalence coupling between
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v)
has coupling index
1
.

Proof.

In view of Lemmas
16.9
and
16.11
, the groups
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v)
are isomorphic, so it is enough to show that every self measure equivalence coupling of
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)
has coupling index
1
.

Recall that since
v\in VY_{G}
, the stabilizer
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)
is a vertex group of
G
(for its graph product structure). Hypothesis
(\mathrm{H}_{1})
gives us a finite-index extension
\widehat{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v)
.

We first observe that every self measure equivalence coupling
\hat{\Sigma}_{v}
of
\widehat{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v)
has coupling index
1
. Indeed, since
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)
has finite index in
\widehat{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v)
, the space
\hat{\Sigma}_{v}
is also a self measure equivalence coupling of
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)
. So Hypothesis
(\mathrm{H}_{1})
yields a Borel
(\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)\times\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v))
-equivariant map
\theta_{v}:\hat{\Sigma}_{v}\to\widehat{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v)
. Since
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)
is ICC (Remark
16.3
) and normal in
\widehat{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v)
, it follows from
[
Kid10
, Lemma 5.8]
that
\theta_{v}
is in fact
(\widehat{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v)\times\widehat{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v))
-equivariant. Then
\theta_{v}^{-1}(\{1\})
is a common Borel fundamental domain for the two actions of
\widehat{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v)
on
\hat{\Sigma}_{v}
, so
\hat{\Sigma}_{v}
has coupling index
1
.

Let now
\Sigma_{v}
be a self measure equivalence coupling of
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)
. Starting from
\Sigma_{v}
and from a measure equivalence coupling between
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)
and
\widehat{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v)
of coupling index
[\widehat{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v):{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v)]
, by composition of couplings, one obtains a self measure equivalence coupling of
\widehat{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(v)
with the same coupling index as
\Sigma_{v}
(see e.g. Item c on page 300 of
[
Fur11
]
, below Definition 2.1). It thus follows from the previous paragraph that the coupling index of
\Sigma_{v}
is
1
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 16.13
.

The subcomplex
Y_{G}
is a strict fundamental domain for the
H
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
.

Proof.

Assume towards a contradiction that
Y_{G}
is not a strict fundamental domain for the
H
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
. This means that

—

either two distinct points
x,y
of
Y_{G}
belong to the same
H
-orbit,

—

or there is a point in
\mathbb{D}_{G}
which is not in the
H
-orbit of any point of
Y_{G}
.

We start by excluding the first option. Assuming that
x,y\in Y_{G}
belong to the same
H
-orbit, we will prove that
x=y
. Let
\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y}
be the cubes of minimal dimension that contain
x,y
; these are contained in
Y_{G}
because
Y_{G}
is a subcomplex of
\mathbb{D}_{G}
. And
\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y}
also belong to the same
H
-orbit, in particular they belong to the same
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})
-orbit. In view of Lemma
16.7
, the cubes
\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y}
are in the same
G
-orbit. Since
Y_{G}
is a strict fundamental domain for the
G
-action, it follows that
\sigma_{x}=\sigma_{y}
. But since automorphisms of
\mathbb{D}_{G}
preserve types of vertices, every automorphism that preserves
\sigma_{x}
acts as the identity on
\sigma_{x}
. It follows that
x=y
, as desired. The first option is excluded.

We now assume that there exists a point
x
in
\mathbb{D}_{G}
which is not in the
H
-orbit of any point of
Y_{G}
, and aim for a contradiction. Let
\sigma
be a cube that contains
x
and contains a rank
0
vertex
v
. Then
\sigma
is not in the
H
-orbit of any cube of
Y_{G}
. Denoting by
v_{0}
the unique rank
0
vertex of
Y_{G}
, it follows that
v
is not in the
H
-orbit of
v_{0}
.

The rank
0
vertices
v_{0}
and
v
can be joined by an edge path, such that vertices along the path alternate between rank
0
and rank
1
vertices (see
fig.

26
for an example). Let
v_{1}
be the last rank
0
vertex along this path that belongs to the
H
-orbit of
v_{0}
, and let
v_{2}
be the next rank
0
vertex along this path. Then
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are rank
0
vertices of
\mathbb{D}_{G}
in distinct
H
-orbits which are adjacent to a common rank
1
vertex
w
.

Figure 26
:
Illustration of a path going from
v_{0}=e
to
v=g_{1}g_{2}
(where
g_{i}\in G_{v_{i}}
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
) such that vertices on the path alternate between rank
1
and
0
.

Recall from Lemma
16.9
that

\Omega_{w}:=\theta^{-1}(\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})}(w))

is a measure equivalence coupling between the stabilizers
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(w)
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(w)
. For every
i\in\{1,2\}
, let

X_{w,i}:=\theta^{-1}(\{f\in\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})}(w)\mid f(v_{i})=v_{1}\}).

The action of
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(w)
on the set of rank
0
vertices adjacent to
w
is nothing but the action of
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(w)
by left translations on itself. In particular it is free and transitive, so for every
i\in\{1,2\}
, the set
X_{w,i}
is a Borel fundamental domain for the action of
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(w)
on
\Omega_{w}
. In particular
m(X_{w,1})=m(X_{w,2})
.

For every
i\in\{1,2\}
, the group
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v_{i})
is trivial (Corollary
16.10
).
Therefore, since
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are not in the same
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(w)
-orbit, the set
X_{w,1}\cup X_{w,2}
meets almost every
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(w)
-orbit in
\Omega_{w}
at most once, and therefore it is contained in a Borel fundamental domain
Y_{w}
for the action of
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(w)
on
\Omega_{w}
. In particular
m(Y_{w})\geq 2m(X_{w,1})
, so the coupling index satisfies

[\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(w):\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(w)]_{\Omega_{w}}\geq 2.

This is a contradiction to Lemma
16.12
, which completes our proof.
∎

16.4
Exploiting the structure of simple complex of groups

In this final step, we will exploit the structure of
H
as a simple complex of groups in the sense of Bridson–Haefliger
[
BH99
, Chapter II.12]
to get a fine control on the nature of the group
H
. We continue with the same general setting as in Section
16.3
, and assume in addition that the group
G
satisfies Hypothesis
(\mathrm{H}_{4})
from Theorem
16.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be the poset of cubes of
\mathbb{D}_{G}
, ordered by inclusion. Then
\mathbb{D}_{G}
has a stratification indexed by
\mathfrak{P}
, whose strata are exactly the cubes of
\mathbb{D}_{G}
: here
stratification
is understood in the sense of
[
BH99
, Definition II.12.1]
, i.e.

—

\mathbb{D}_{G}
is the union of its strata;

—

the intersection of two strata, if non-empty, is again a stratum;

—

for every point in
\mathbb{D}_{G}
, the intersection of all strata that contain
x
is again a stratum (namely, it is the cube of smallest dimension that contains
x
).

The actions of
G
and of
H
on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
are strata-preserving, i.e. every element sends every stratum bijectively onto a stratum. And in view of Lemma
16.13
, the subcomplex
Y_{G}
is a strict fundamental domain for the strata-preserving actions of
G
and
H
in the sense of
[
BH99
, Definition II.12.7]
: every cube of
\mathbb{D}_{G}
has a unique translate in
Y_{G}
.

In addition
\mathbb{D}_{G}
is simply connected (it is even
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
). Therefore, we can apply
[
BH99
, Corollary II.12.22]
and deduce that
H
is the direct limit (amalgam) of the isotropy subgroups of the cubes contained in
Y_{G}
, in the sense of
[
BH99
, Definition II.12.12]
. The meaning of this is the following. Let

\mathfrak{Q}=\{\sigma\in\mathfrak{P}\mid\sigma\subseteq Y_{G}\}.

As before, for every cube
\sigma\subseteq Y_{G}
, we let
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
be the stabilizer of
\sigma
for the
H
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
. Also, given cubes
\tau\subseteq\sigma
both contained in
Y_{G}
, we let
\psi_{\tau\sigma}:\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)\to\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\tau)
be the inclusion. Then
H
is isomorphic to the group obtained from the free product of the groups
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
, with
\sigma
ranging in
\mathfrak{Q}
, by imposing as only extra relations that
\psi_{\tau\sigma}(h)=h
, whenever
\tau\subseteq\sigma
both belong to
\mathfrak{Q}
and
h\in\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
.

We are now in position to prove the main theorem of this section.

Proof of Theorem
16.1
.

Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
be the rank
1
vertices in
Y_{G}
. Let
\sigma\subseteq Y_{G}
be a cube, and let
w
be its minimal vertex. Let
v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{k}}
be the rank
1
vertices that satisfy
v_{i_{j}}\leq w
(where the partial order
\leq
is just the inclusion of the cosets represented by these vertices). We say that
v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{k}}
are the rank
1
vertices
below

\sigma
(notice that in the case where
\sigma
contains the rank
0
vertex of
Y_{G}
, there are no rank
1
vertices below
\sigma
). For every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, there is a cube
\sigma^{\prime}_{j}
in
Y_{G}
that contains
v_{i_{j}}
as its minimal vertex, and also contains
w
. So Corollary
16.8
implies that, for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we have

\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})}(v_{i_{j}})=\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})}(\sigma^{\prime}_{j})\subseteq\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})}(\sigma)=\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{D}_{G})}(w).

Therefore the
H
-stabilizer of
v_{i_{j}}
is equal to
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma^{\prime}_{j})
, and is contained in
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(w)=\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
.

By Lemmas
16.9
and
16.11
, we already know that the stabilizer
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
is abstractly isomorphic to
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\sigma)
, which splits as
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\sigma)=G_{v_{i_{1}}}\times\dots\times G_{v_{i_{k}}}
(here we naturally identify the rank
1
vertices
v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{k}}
with vertices of the defining graph
\Gamma
, and the groups
G_{v_{i_{j}}}
are exactly vertex groups for the graph product structure of
G
).

In addition, for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we have an inclusion map
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v_{i_{j}})\to\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
, and
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v_{i_{j}})
is isomorphic to
G_{v_{i_{j}}}
(
lemmas

16.9
and
16.11
). It thus follows from Hypothesis
(\mathrm{H}_{4})
that the image of
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v_{i_{j}})
in
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
is exactly equal to

\{1\}\times\dots\times\{1\}\times G_{v_{i_{j}}}\times\{1\}\times\dots\times\{1\},

under the above identification between
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(\sigma)
. Therefore
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
splits as the direct product
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}({v_{i_{1}}})\times\dots\times\mathrm{Stab}_{H}({v_{i_{k}}})
, in such a way that the inclusion maps
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}({v_{i_{j}}})\to\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)
are just factor inclusions.

In other words, given any finite collection of rank
1
vertices
v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{k}}
spanning a clique when viewed as vertices of
\Gamma
, the subgroup generated by
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v_{i_{1}}),\dots,\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v_{i_{k}})
is equal to their direct product, and it is also equal to the stabilizer of any cube
\sigma
such that the collection of rank
1
vertices below
\sigma
is exactly
\{v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{k}}\}
. From this, it follows that whenever
\sigma,\tau
are two cubes with
\tau\subseteq\sigma
, the inclusion map
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\sigma)\to\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(\tau)
is a factor inclusion.

It follows that the direct limit of the groups
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v_{i})
is nothing but their graph product over
\Gamma
, and it follows from
[
BH99
, Corollary II.12.22]
that this direct limit is isomorphic to
H
. Since
\mathrm{Stab}_{H}(v_{i})
is isomorphic to
G_{v_{i}}
, this shows that
H
is isomorphic to
G
. ∎

17
An ME-rigid group which is not QI-rigid

In this section, we provide an example of a graph product that satisfies all assumptions from Theorem
16.1
. Our example will involve Higman groups
[
Hig51
]
, defined for
k\geq 4
as

\mathrm{Hig}_{k}=\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\mid a_{i}a_{i+1}a_{i}^{-1}=a_{i+1}^{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\forall i\penalty\ (\text{mod\penalty\ }k)\rangle.

Our construction will enable us to prove the following theorem, which we recall from the introduction.

Theorem 1.16
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite simple graph with no transvection and no partial conjugation, such that
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)=\{1\}
. Let
(k_{v})_{v\in V\Gamma}
be a sequence of integers at least
5
, such that
k_{v}
does not divide
k_{w}
whenever
v\neq w
. Let
G
be the graph product over
\Gamma
with vertex groups
(\mathrm{Hig}_{k_{v}})_{v\in V\Gamma}
. Then

1.

If a torsion-free countable group
H
is measure equivalent to
G
, then
H
is isomorphic to
G
.

2.

There exists an infinite family of torsion-free countable groups that are all quasi-isometric to
G
, and pairwise not commensurable.

17.1
Homomorphisms between Higman groups

The following proposition is an elaboration on work of Martin
[
Mar17
]
, who proved the second assertion in the case where
k=4
. We will follow his proof very closely.

Proposition 17.1
.

Let
k,k^{\prime}\geq 4
. Then

—

There exists a non-trivial homomorphism
\mathrm{Hig}_{k^{\prime}}\to\mathrm{Hig}_{k}
if and only if
k
divides
k^{\prime}
.

—

Every non-trivial homomorphism
\mathrm{Hig}_{k}\to\mathrm{Hig}_{k}
is an automorphism.

Proof.

We let
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\}
be a standard generating set of
\mathrm{Hig}_{k}
, and
\{a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\}
be a standard generating set of
\mathrm{Hig}_{k^{\prime}}
(as in their standard presentations).

If
k
divides
k^{\prime}
, then the map sending the generator
a^{\prime}_{i}
of
\mathrm{Hig}_{k^{\prime}}
to the generator
a_{i}
of
\mathrm{Hig}_{k}
(with the index
i
considered modulo
k
), extends to a non-trivial homomorphism from
\mathrm{Hig}_{k^{\prime}}
to
\mathrm{Hig}_{k}
.

Conversely, assume that there exists a non-trivial homomorphism
\theta:\mathrm{Hig}_{k^{\prime}}\to\mathrm{Hig}_{k}
. We will prove that
k
divides
k^{\prime}
, and that if
k=k^{\prime}
then
\theta
is an isomorphism, which will establish the proposition. Most of the proof follows
[
Mar17
, Section 3]
very closely. Martin’s proof was written when
k=k^{\prime}=4
, but the arguments readily extend to all
k,k^{\prime}\geq 4
; we will provide a guide through the proof.

First, observe that
\theta
cannot send any generator of
\mathrm{Hig}_{k^{\prime}}
to
1
, as otherwise the defining relations of
\mathrm{Hig}_{k^{\prime}}
would imply that every generator is sent to
1
, contradicting that
\theta
is non-trivial.

As in
[
Mar17
, Section 1.1]
or
[
Mar21
, Section 5]
, the group
\mathrm{Hig}_{k}
(resp.
\mathrm{Hig}_{k^{\prime}}
) acts on a
\mathrm{CAT}(0)
polygonal complex
X_{k}
(resp.
X_{k^{\prime}}
) with strict fundamental domain a
2
-dimensional
k
-gon (resp. a
2
-dimensional
k^{\prime}
-gon). For both actions, stabilizers of
2
-dimensional cells are trivial, edge stabilizers are isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
(conjugate to the cyclic subgroups generated by the generators
a_{i}
or
a^{\prime}_{i}
), and vertex stabilizers are isomorphic to
\mathrm{BS}(1,2)
(conjugate to the subgroups of the form
\langle a_{i},a_{i+1}\rangle
, or
\langle a^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i+1}\rangle
, with indices understood modulo
k
or
k^{\prime}
). Moreover, for every edge
e=vw
of
X_{k}
, exactly one of the homomorphisms
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v)
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(w)
has undistorted image. As in
[
Mar17
, Section 2.1]
, we orient the edge towards the vertex
w
such that the inclusion
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(w)
is undistorted. If
e
is oriented towards
w
, and
e^{\prime}
is any other edge incident on
w
, then
\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e)\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(e^{\prime})=\{1\}
: this is the contents of
[
Mar17
, Lemma 2.1]
. In particular, as in
[
Mar17
, Corollary 2.3]
, the fixed point set
\mathrm{Fix}(h)
of any non-trivial element of
\mathrm{Hig}_{k}
is always contained in the outward star of a vertex, i.e. in the union of all edges emanating from that vertex (
\mathrm{Fix}(h)
can be empty or reduced to one vertex).

As in
[
Mar17
, Lemmas 2.4 and 3.6]
(with the same proof), the homomorphism
\theta
sends every subgroup
\langle a^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i+1}\rangle
into a unique subgroup of the form
g\langle a_{j},a_{j+1}\rangle g^{-1}
. In particular
\theta
induces a map
\theta_{\ast}:VX_{k^{\prime}}\to VX_{k}
. As in
[
Mar17
, Lemma 3.7]
, the map
\theta_{\ast}
extends to an orientation-preserving map from the
1
-skeleton
X^{1}_{k^{\prime}}
to the
1
-skeleton
X^{1}_{k}
. And
\theta_{\ast}
does not collapse any edge to a vertex (see the second paragraph of the proof of
[
Mar17
, Lemma 3.8]
).

Consider the vertices
